- **M3 in three parts (2026-10-04):** M3's contents need about seven decisions expensive to reverse: goods and the ledger, property claims, knowledge, building components, terrain edits, weather and soils, and the execution modes. That is more than one milestone's three ADRs (§1 rule 7). It is split into M3a (village economy), M3b (knowledge and building) and M3c (seasons and time), each with the usable bar and a demo of its own; M3's demo is M3a's. The economy comes first because the rest builds on it: techniques unlock recipes, buildings hold stores and workshops, weather moves prices.
- **Goods in their units (2026-10-04, M3a slice H, ADR-0006 §1):** stores keep one number per good, by content id, in the good's unit: kilograms, or for a tool its standard tools' worth of use (`life_h` hours each). An hour's use takes `1 / life_h` of a tool, so 2.4 sickles are two and what is left of a third; a tool made with skill adds more than one. Each good says how it is eaten (`raw`, `cooked`, `never`), and what a good is kept back from (seed from grain) replaces the `reserve` flag. Content API 7.
- **Grain is ground or pounded before it is eaten (2026-10-04, slice H):** threshed grain is not eaten as it is. At a saddle quern it gives 0.98 kg of wholemeal flour a kilogram at 0.5 h/kg for a grinder of middling skill (research 08-03 §2.1: experiments 0.77–3.33 h/kg, ethnographic grinders 0.25–0.50; with skill, 0.83 down to 0.38). Flour is baked into flatbread on the hearth: a quarter hour to set the fire and 0.3 h a kilogram of bread (08-03 §2.2: a 5 kg batch in 1–3 hours), with 0.5 kg of firewood a kilogram of flour (07-06 §2.3), so the people profile's daily firewood is 0.3 kg a person lower than M1's. Without a quern, grain is pounded in a mortar for porridge at 1 h/kg (a tuning value; 07-04 §6.5: non-bread grain foods must stay possible). Seed goes into the quern only in hunger, and never the seed for the ground already cropped. Households make ready food when what is ready (bread, porridge, provisions) falls below 2 days of their needs, and grind ahead when that and their flour fall below 3 days (grinding was a near-daily chore), and only as much as brings it back (tuning values).
- **NUDGE: what is made ahead spoils (2026-10-04, slice H):** with bread's half-life at 4 days and households baking at least a day's bread whenever they baked, the test village of seed 2 used two thirds more food than in M1 from the same harvests, and in its third year 31 of 43 people left before the harvest; with spoilage of bread and flour turned off, the same village used 5 % more than in M1. Households now make only what brings their ready food back to its days. *Revised:* flatbread's half-life is 30 days (thin flatbread dries rather than goes off and keeps for weeks) and flour is ground at most 3 days ahead, both tuning values. With 8 days and a week, bread and flour going off took about a tenth of all a village ate, and the first ten-year smoke of slice H, with the tool wants below, kept only 3 of its 10 villages. Now about 7 % of a village's food is lost on its way to the mouth: in store, at the quern and as bread and flour go off.
- **Planning for what is lost (2026-10-04, slice H):** a household grows its food over one less `loss_share` (0.1), the grain lost before it is eaten (research 08-01 §2.3 tests 5, 10 and 20 % a year for routine losses from dry stores). The test villages lost about 7 % this way, and grinding and pounding are hard work that people eat for. It seldom binds: a household can break only about a quarter hectare of new ground a year in the sowing window, so villages stay short of the area they plan while they grow.
- **A stock path to the harvest (2026-10-04, slice H):** a household counts the days until its next harvest begins to come in (its first sown field ripening, or the crop's calendar, and a week to reap and thresh the first of it) and wants food for them and 30 days more (research 08-01 §2.3: a seasonal reserve until the next reliable food, plus 0–90 days; 08-05 §1.5: a grain holder plans a stock path to the next harvest). Stores short of that make wild food worth going out for at half the weight of a shortage at hand (`w_lean`, a tuning value; the reason *stores will not last to the harvest*). Before, a household felt a shortage only within 5 days of running out, and sat at the hearth through the spring while its grain ran down toward the hungry gap. In the test village examined (river valley, seed 3), 31 of 44 people then left in the fourth spring; with the outlook 20 of 45 left and 8 died, but the hours spent on wild food over the year barely changed, so the difference is within what separates one run from the next. The hungry gap stays (see the NUDGE on the bread chain's cost below).
- **Goods are accounted for (2026-10-04, slice H, ADR-0006 §3):** every change to a household's stores is counted as a flow of its good (brought, gathered or harvested, made, used, eaten, spoiled, burned, worn, built, sown, or taken away by a household that left); moving goods between households is neither. A test over a world's first season and the ten-year smoke at each year's end check that what households hold changed by exactly what came in less what went out. The counters are not saved: a loaded world's books open at the load.
- **Tools are needed, worn and made (2026-10-04, slice H):** reaping and cutting reeds need a sickle (flint blades in a haft, about 100 hours of use), preparing a field a hoe (150 h; weeds are pulled by hand), cutting poles and building an axe (200 h), and grinding a quern (6,000 h: ten years and more of a household's grinding). These lifetimes are tuning values; the research gives none, only that worn tools slow work (11-12 §1.4). Work without a free tool is left out, and the tool then counts as one work waits on. Reaping by hand is the one fallback, at 0.6 of a sickle's rate, and only while no sickle is free. Households want a sickle and a hoe for each member old enough for the work that needs it (a sickle from 10, when children cut reeds; a hoe from 12) and an axe and a quern for every two, with a quarter of a tool to spare, and make them: a sickle from 0.3 kg of flint and 0.5 kg of wood in 3 hours, an axe from 1.5 kg of stone and a kilogram of wood in 15 (07-05 §2.2: 5–40 h for a ground-stone axe head), a hoe from 1.5 kg of wood in 2 hours (with a flint flake if there is no axe), a quern from 30 kg of stone in 20. At first households wanted tools only for their adults and weeding needed a hoe, so children could no longer weed and crops lost more to weeds. Founding families bring the tools they want; a new couple takes a share of their households' tools.
- **Skills by practice (2026-10-04, slice H, ADR-0006 §2):** a person's level in a skill (milling, baking, knapping, stoneworking, woodworking) rises with each hour of practice by s′ = s + (1 − s)(1 − e^(−kE)), k = ln 5 / T80 (06-08 §5.2), with T80 150 hours for grinding and baking and 800–1,500 hours for the crafts (tuning values within 06-08's routine and broad-craft classes). Skill sets speed (0.5–0.7 at level 0, 1.2–1.4 at mastery) and a tool's life (0.4–0.5 to 1.5–1.6). Grown founders bring levels drawn evenly from each skill's range; unpractised skills do not fade (07-02 §2.3).
- **Stone and flint lie where they lie (2026-10-04, slice H):** a land resource can be a deposit, a stock per hectare by habitat laid down once that never grows back: loose stone (most on uplands, cobbles on floodplains) and flint nodules. Gathered like any resource, only while the household needs them for a tool. A stand-in for geology, which arrives with M3b's deposits (research 03-05).
- **Wire 1.8, save schema 9 and content API 7 (2026-10-04, slice H):** the Welcome lists skills, and each good says how it is eaten and, for a tool, how long one lasts; PersonInfo carries a person's skills and the household's days of food ready to eat (additive). Saves keep each person's skills by skill id. Saves of schema 8 and earlier load with each household given the tools a founding family of its size and ages would bring and each person skills drawn as a founder's are. Content gains the `recipe` and `skill` kinds.
- **`civ-host run` (2026-10-04):** lives one world for some years and reports at each year's end its people, harvest, stores, tools, skills, how an adult's day is spent and what became of its food (gathered and harvested, eaten, spoiled, sown, put into recipes and made). A calibration tool, not a test.
- **NUDGE: idle hours (2026-10-04):** in the M1 calibration, as now, an adult of the test villages spends about 3 hours a day on production, 1–2 on domestic work, 6 sitting at the hearth and 4–5 resting. Research 04-02 §2.4 proposes about 6 hours of production and 4 uncommitted for an ordinary farming day, from task requirements rather than a fixed workday. The tasks simply need little time once the fields are broken; work for others and for trade (slices I and J) should draw on these hours. Revisit if labour stays slack once there are wages.
- **NUDGE: the bread chain's cost (2026-10-04, slice H):** grinding and baking take each adult about an hour a day, and about 7 % of a village's food is lost on its way to the mouth; M1's people ate their grain as it was, losing only what spoiled in store. Villages are more fragile than M1's. In the ten-year smoke (villages keeping at least 10 people): M1 kept 8 of 10 in two runs; slice H's first run kept 3; with bread and flour keeping longer, 6; with every measure above, 6 again (41, 30, 41, 54, 48 and 55 people; the four others fell to 8, 8, 3 and 0), with every good accounted for in every year of all ten worlds. The failures examined are the hungry gap of the fourth to ninth spring: a village that grew outruns the ground it can break (about a quarter hectare a household a year in the sowing window, planned at 6 working hours a day), its stores run out before the harvest, and households leave together. Research 04-02 §2.4 puts a farmer's production day at 6 hours ordinarily and 10 at a peak; the people here spend about 3 hours a day on production and sit at the hearth or rest for 10 (see the NUDGE on idle hours). Harder work at the peaks and in the hungry gap, livestock, a second crop and help between settlements are the levers to revisit; exchange (slice I) and hired labour (J) may help too, by moving food to where it is short.
- **One ledger between households (2026-10-04, M3a slice I, ADR-0006 §3):** every movement of goods between households is one transfer whose legs all move or none do (a giver must cover everything it gives), counted by channel: gift (asking for food), share (a kill shared out), household share (a new couple's share of their families' goods), inheritance (the goods of a household that is no more pass to the one its people join), barter and sale. Channel codes are fixed for saves and the boundary. The conservation check counts each leg as given by one household and received by the other, so the books still balance.
- **What a good is worth: hours of a household's own work (2026-10-04, slice I):** a household values each good at what one more unit would cost it in hours of its own work: grain by its field work over a cautious yield less the seed, and threshing; a gathered good by the best return of the places it knows, with a quarter more for the walk (half the best rate a resource gives for a good it has never gone out for); a made good by its recipe at the household's best skill, with the tools it wears; food it cannot get itself (provisions) by its energy, at the cost of its cheapest food. Research 08-04 §1.2 anchors prices in a smoothed normal cost; 08-15 §2.1 keeps work time per unit beside prices. The walk's quarter and the half rate are tuning values.
- **Posted terms, reviewed weekly (2026-10-04, slice I, ADR-0006 §4):** each household reviews on its own day of the week what it can spare: tools beyond its members' wants and spare; food beyond what sees it to the harvest, 30 days more and a quarter over, and only food that keeps a year (grain, not bread); materials once its home is finished; never firewood; at least half a tool or 5 kg. It asks its own cost and 25 % (08-04 §1.2), moving each review half the way toward that anchor, nudged up by demand nobody met and down when nothing sold, by at most 5 % (08-04 §4.3: 1–5 %). Its terms are in up to four goods it takes in payment: those it is shortest of first, then what its neighbours already pay in, what keeps longest and what is worth most for its weight (08-06 §1.3: familiarity, durability, portability), or its settlement's money. A price by weight must be one a person can carry for a unit, and a price in a tool must come to at least half a tool: a worn tool changes hands, not a splinter of one. At first tools were paid in tenths, and sellers' terms filled up with fractions of axes and querns.
- **Buying from a neighbour (2026-10-04, slice I):** trade is an activity like any other. A person walks to the seller whose terms save their household the most hours of its own work over getting the good itself, the walk there and back included (08-05 §1.3–1.4: buyers compare the few sellers they know by price and walk; one bounded exchange, no haggling), for a tool it needs or food it is short of. The buyer pays out of what it holds beyond what it keeps, valued at its own cost and how much it wants it. The deal settles at the seller's door through the ledger, as barter or, paid in the settlement's money, as a sale, and the offer shrinks by what was sold. Making a tool that costs less from a neighbour is left out (*it costs less from a neighbour*).
- **Recorded demand and making to sell (2026-10-04, slice I):** a household that needs a tool nobody in its settlement offers puts its need on record at each review (ADR-0006 §4), weighted so that what the market remembers comes to the want itself, a level rather than a count of reviews. A household that does not need that tool makes one to sell when others want it and would give more hours of their own work than its cost and margin; making it is worth the share of what they would give that the household keeps (08-04 §1.2: produce for funded demand, not for sales already made; reason *others want it*). At first every review recorded a whole tool, and a village of eight households showed 38 axes wanted after two months when each lacked only its spare quarter.
- **Acceptance and the inferred money (2026-10-04, slice I, ADR-0006 §4):** each settlement's market remembers, per good, the worth of the payments settled in it, fading by half every 30 days (08-04 §4.3: demand memory 14–56 days). Its money is the good that settles at least half of that worth over at least 10 remembered trades (08-06 §2.3: acceptance beliefs from 10–100 payments per half-life, here the fewest). Nothing in the engine names a money good, and a settlement may never have one. Once a good is money every seller takes it, which is how acceptance feeds on itself (08-06 §1.3). The section the ADR calls `economy` is split by subject: saves gain a `market` section now, and firms will have their own.
- **Firewood first (2026-10-04, slice I, an observation):** in the test village of seed 3 (river valley), nobody had grain to spare in the first spring, and households paid for hoes and sickles with firewood: every household burns it and holds some beyond what it keeps, it never spoils and it divides finely. With 5 remembered trades as the bar, it was the village's money within two months, on a dozen trades; with the bar at 10, it settled 95 % of what was paid after two months but on too few trades to be money. A plausible commodity money for a village where the walk to a neighbour is short (08-06 §1.3: grain serves local obligations and travels poorly, and firewood more so), but it is not one the research names. Watch whether grain takes over once harvests leave some to spare.
- **Wire 1.9 and save schema 10 (2026-10-04, slice I):** `GetMarkets` answers with each settlement's market: barter or its money and a summary in words; per good, what is offered and by how many households, what sold, what was wanted with none on offer, its share of payments and the last terms; every household's terms; the latest trades in words; and a monthly price history (what a unit was worth to its sellers, in hours of their own work). The snapshot's `markets_rev` changes with them. Saves keep each household's terms and each settlement's market: its tallies, last terms, latest trades and twenty years of monthly price history. Saves of schema 9 and earlier load with no markets, and households post their terms at their next review.
- **Exchange in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, slice I):** with exchange, 8 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people (on the coast 16, 45, 10 and 58; in the river valley 58, 38, 51 and 55); the bands of seed 1 fell to 6 and 3, as they did in M1. Slice H's last run kept 6. One run each, and runs differ, so this does not show that exchange holds villages together. Every good was accounted for in every year of all ten worlds.
- **Household workshops (2026-10-04, M3a slice J, ADR-0006 §5):** a firm is a household's workshop, an account with stores of its own inside the household that owns it (research 08-08 §1.1: an early enterprise can be an activity account within a household). One opens when someone chooses to make a tool to sell (slice I's *others want it*): the household's first such session opens a workshop for that good, named for whoever set it up ("Wren's sickle workshop"), and a household keeps one workshop a good. Its owners put in what each session uses, through the ledger's `owner` channel, and work for it unpaid; what is made is the workshop's. It posts terms for its stock as its owners would for theirs, on their review day, sells from its own stores and is paid into them. At each review its owners draw out what it was paid, beyond what it keeps to pay wages, and any of its goods they now lack themselves (08-08 §1.6: owner withdrawals compete with reinvestment). A workshop whose household is no more passes with its people to the household they join. Goods held by workshops are counted in the conservation check with households'.
- **Making to sell, revised (2026-10-04, slice J):** slice I's trigger, wants on record that nobody meets, stops once one seller offers the tool, and starts again only when that offer is gone and buyers record their want again at their next review. A household now also makes a tool to sell when it sold lately in its settlement and nobody offers it now; making it is worth what it last sold for, in hours of the sellers' work. A workshop's stock counts as its owners' spare, so they do not make more while it holds unsold tools.
- **Books (2026-10-04, slice J, ADR-0006 §5):** a workshop keeps its latest 64 entries (`book_entries`), each a good moved by kind with the household on the other side: put in, drawn out, made, used, sold, taken in payment, paid in wages and lost. It also keeps a statement for every month of its life: the amounts by kind and good; the hours worked for it by its owners and by people it hired; its income (what its payments were worth), its costs (what it used and paid in wages) and its stock at the month's end. Worth is in hours of its owners' own work, what the goods would cost them to get, as every household reckons (slice I), not in a settlement's money, which most do not have (08-08 §5.3: inventories, income, expenses and owner claims are vocabulary enough).
- **Why workshops close (2026-10-04, slice J):** a workshop that has sold nothing for 180 days (`idle_close_days`, a tuning value) is given up (a voluntary exit), one whose household is no more and passes to nobody closes (the owner's line ended), and one that cannot pay a wage it owes, its owners' stores included, fails (08-08 §1.8 keeps exit causes apart; there are no ventures yet, so no completion; §2.5: no closure hazard beyond the causes modelled). What it holds goes to its owners, or is left behind when there are none. The chronicle notes each opening, and each closing with its cause. With no credit or debts in M3a, a wage that cannot be paid closes a workshop at once, though 08-08 §1.8 would first allow arrears and a workout; the worker keeps what was paid. Revisit with credit.
- **Hired labour at posted wages (2026-10-04, slice J, ADR-0006 §5):** at each review a workshop plans the work it would hire until the next: what buyers want of its goods beyond its stock (wants nobody met, and a review's share of what sold lately), in hours of its recipe, at most 21 hours (`max_hire_hours`, a tuning value: one person's three hours a day). It posts a wage: half (`wage_share`, a tuning value; the research gives no share of labour) of what an hour of work adds at middling skill, its terms less what the work uses, in hours of its owners' work. It pays in the settlement's money, or else in the good neighbours most accept that it or its owners can spare, never a tool. While hours go untaken, the wage rises by 5 % at each wage review, every 14 days (08-10 §5.6: employer wage reviews every 14–30 days, 1–5 % a review), but never above all an hour adds, so hired work never costs a workshop more than it brings. Work for hire is an activity like any other, the new behaviour `hire` (08-10 §1.3: a wage is weighed against what it displaces; §5.3: bounded local matching over posted offers). A person weighs the best workshop within reach by what a session pays, as food for a hungry household or as goods, against the walk and the effort. The worker makes the workshop's goods at its owners' home with its tools, and is paid for the time through the ledger's `wage` channel; the owners cover any shortfall. New reasons: *what the work is paid* and *nobody nearby is hiring*.
- **NUDGE: hired work is rare (2026-10-04, slice J):** in four three-year runs (river valley, seeds 2, 3, 5 and 7 on 6 km maps), 9 sickle and hoe workshops opened in three of the villages. Together they made about 17 tools and sold 8, and every one was given up after months without a sale. They hired 27 hours of work in all (6 in one village, 21 in another), while an adult spends some 10 hours a day resting or at the hearth. Wages are low because terms sit near cost: a workshop asks its owners' cost and a quarter, so an hour of hired work adds little more than what the hour costs, and half of that is offered. In the example world built for the observer's tests, a sickle workshop paid 0.18 kg of grain an hour, worth 0.3 h of its owners' own work. People who can farm or gather for themselves do better at home, and mostly the hungry take the work. That is plausible where every household can make the tools it needs (08-10 §1.3), but it is not calibrated. Revisit with property regimes (K) and larger villages (L), which may leave households without land or materials, and if labour stays slack (see the NUDGE on idle hours).
- **Runs differ by world, and determinism is tested from a save (2026-10-04, slice J):** two worlds made from one seed draw their own identities, which order the events of an instant, so they live different lives (as the README's calibration notes say). The determinism test therefore loads one save twice and checks that 30 days later both save to the same bytes, section by section. Tests of behaviour that emerges (a workshop opening, a wage taken) set up its conditions, as the firm tests and the observer's example world do, rather than hope a seed brings them.
- **Wire 1.10 and save schema 11 (2026-10-04, slice J):** `GetFirms` lists every workshop in brief: its name, owners and settlement, open or closed and why, what it makes, the hours it would hire and its record in words. `GetFirm` gives a workshop's page: who set it up and since when its owners have had it, its last sale, what it holds, its terms, its wage in words, its latest book entries in words and every month's statement. The snapshot's `firms_rev` changes with them. Chronicle spans can link a workshop; market offers and trades say when the seller is a workshop, and the market counts the workshops among the sellers. Saves keep each workshop's record, stores, terms, wage and books, and the chronicle keeps which workshop an entry is about. Saves of schema 10 and earlier load with no workshops. Content gains a `[firm]` table in the people profile and the `hire` behaviour (`core:activity/hired_work`). `cargo run --release -p civ-sim --example workshop_world -- <saves>` builds the world with a workshop at work that the observer's browser test loads; like the kernel tests, it sets up the conditions (skills, stores and a want kept on record) and lets the workshop arise.
- **Workshops in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, slice J):** with workshops and hired labour, all ten smoke worlds passed, and 7 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people (on the coast 22, 40 and 59; in the river valley 56, 48, 30 and 55). The bands of seed 1 fell to 4 and 0, as in every run since M1, and the coast band of seed 4 fell to 8 in its tenth year (58 in slice I's run). Slice I's run kept 8. One run each, and runs differ. Every good, workshops' included, was accounted for in every year of all ten worlds.
- **Holders and users (2026-10-04, M3a slice K, ADR-0007 §1):** every field records who holds it, a household or a settlement, besides the household that works it. Holding changes nothing physical: a change of holder leaves a field's ground, crop and yield as they were (research 08-09: title neither creates nor destroys land or crops). Dwelling plots and huts stay with the household that lives in them under every regime. Fields change hands at a review only between crops (fallow, no work begun toward the next crop, nothing waiting to be threshed), so a crop and the work put into it stay with the household that did it; at a death a crop goes with its field, as part of the estate.
- **Regimes are content (2026-10-04, slice K, ADR-0007 §2):** a `regime` kind (content API 8) says who holds broken ground (`breaker` or `settlement`), how its use is given (`holder` or `need`), the yearly review's date, succession (`heir`, `divided` or `settlement`), whether a couple's household takes a share of its families' fields, and whether use may be let and on what terms. The core pack has two, household fields (the default) and village fields; exactly one is the default. A world is made under one (the new-world dialog, `civ-host new` and `run --regime`) and keeps it for its life in its saves; a world whose regime the loaded content no longer has runs under the content's default. Their names are content, not states the engine switches between (plan §1).
- **Village fields: allocation by need (2026-10-04, slice K, research 08-09 §5.2, 08-11):** the settlement holds every field its households break. At its yearly review (15 February, before field preparation begins in March; the date is a tuning value) and whenever a household forms, ends or leaves, the households short of the area they plan to crop for their size (the same need they plan by themselves), the furthest short first by share of it, take fields that are vacant or beyond what the household working them needs, never leaving that household short, the nearest their home first. Fields of a household that is no more go back to the settlement. Nobody lets land.
- **Household fields: occupation, succession and shares (2026-10-04, slice K, 08-09 §1.2, §1.7, 08-14 §2.4):** a household holds the ground it breaks. Ground whose holder and user are both no more is vacant; at the next review a household short of land takes it up and holds it (first occupation), and a field whose tenant is gone goes back to its holder. When a household is no more, its fields are divided among its heirs' households, whole fields as near equal in area as they can be (the largest first, each to the heir with least so far). Its heirs are the households of its last member's nearest living kin, at the first step out where any is found, and only those in its own settlement when there are any, so land is not divided among households too far away to work it. Its goods still go to one heir, the household its people join. A couple setting up its own household takes the share of each family's fields that it takes of its stores (its members over the family's), the fields nearest its home first, to the nearest whole field.
- **Leases and rent (2026-10-04, slice K, ADR-0007 §3, 08-09 §1.4, §2.1):** under household fields, at a review a holder with more ground than it needs lets a field, when no vacant ground is left, to a household short of land, for a crop year (`term_years`). The tenant pays the holder a quarter of the grain it threshes from it (`holder_share`: Operation Barga gave landlords 25 % where tenants brought the non-labour inputs; a legal anchor, not a calibrated value), in grain and then seed grain, through the ledger's new `rent` channel (code 9), so the conservation check covers it. At the first review after its term a lease is renewed while the holder still has what it needs and the tenant would be short without the field; otherwise the field goes back to its holder. A tenant that inherits the field it rents holds it. There is no land market.
- **Wealth measures (2026-10-04, slice K, ADR-0007 §4, 08-14 §1.1, §5.6):** the kernel measures each household by land held and land worked (with what it lets and rents), its goods, and the floor area under its roofs. Goods are its store and its open workshops' stock, valued in hours of work at its settlement's prices: per good, the median of what it costs the settlement's households in their own work (0 for a good none of them can price). Ginis of goods and land are person-weighted per head: each member counts at the household's measure over its members (08-14 §1.1; the weighted Gini of §5.6, exact for equal shares within a household). Floor area's Gini is house by house, over the households with a roof of their own, as archaeologists compare houses. It also reports the richest tenth's share of goods (a household on the line counts in part), the shares of households that hold and that work no land, goods and land worked per head, floor area per house, and the land the settlement holds. Each settlement's measures are recorded at each year's end (1 January) and saved. They are never inputs to behaviour: house size by wealth (slice L) will be the first. Income, consumption, debts, mobility and a decomposition of changes (08-14 §5.6) are not measured yet.
- **Wire 1.11 and save schema 12 (2026-10-04, slice K):** saves keep the world's regime, each field's holder and lease, and each settlement's yearly wealth measures. The wealth section is read when present, since saves made earlier in the slice have none and nothing depends on it. Older saves load under the content's default regime with every field held by the household that works it. The wire gains the regimes in the Welcome, each with its rules in sentences rendered by the kernel; `NewWorld.regime_id`; the world's regime; each field's holder and lease; `GetWealth` (each settlement's measures as they stand, its households' and its yearly history); and the snapshot's `wealth_rev`, which changes monthly, when households form or end and when a year is recorded. `fields_rev` now also changes when a field changes hands.
- **Two regimes, three years (2026-10-04, slice K, an observation):** seed 2 (river valley, 6 km map) lived three years under each regime. Under household fields every household held land; under village fields the village held it all (6.5 ha after the first year, 13.5 ha after the third) and one household worked none at the first year's end. The other measures barely differed: the Gini of goods was 0.04, 0.13 and 0.25 at the three years' ends under household fields and 0.05, 0.12 and 0.20 under village fields; of land worked, 0.12–0.14 and 0.13–0.15; of floor area, 0.17–0.18 in both; the richest tenth had 12–22 % and 11–19 % of the goods. With land plentiful nobody let a field, and every household works about what it needs. Whether the regimes diverge with crowding, generations of inheritance and house size by wealth is for slice L's demo to look for, not to force.
- **The last water before dark (2026-10-04, found by the smoke):** the ten-year smoke failed one world in its first month (river valley, seed 3) because a household had no water at midnight. Traced: a couple set up its own household at midday, taking a member's share of its family's water (a few hours' worth, as every household's water runs low by midday and is fetched in the afternoon); both had just gone out to the fields for the afternoon, and when they came home at dusk a meal outweighed the trip for water, and after the meal it was dark. Water is not fetched in the dark, so the household was dry until morning. The same happened on slice J's build (in one of eight worlds), so slice K did not cause it. Now, in the last of the light, once what is left would not see a meal and then the trip, a household whose water will not last the night fetches it before anything that can wait until dark: its shortage counts as much as the strongest hunger and a full shortage together (`w_hunger × max_hunger_drive + w_water`), from the weights already in content. Earlier in the last three hours it still counts as a full shortage, as before. In ten fresh worlds of that seed no household was dry at any day's end of the first month, against three of eight before. Running dry has no other effect in the model (there is no thirst yet); the smoke treats it as a sign that water is not being fetched.
- **Slice K in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** with property regimes and wealth measures (all smoke worlds under the default household fields), nine of ten worlds passed and the tenth failed its first month on the water gap above. With the water fix all ten passed, and 7 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people (on the coast 11, 41 and 54; in the river valley 56, 48, 55 and 10); the bands of seed 1 fell to 4 and 0, and the coast band of seed 4 to 0 in its tenth year. Slice J's run kept 7. One run each, and runs differ. Every good was accounted for in every year of all ten worlds.
- **Faster villages, exactly (2026-10-04, M3a slice L):** a village of 200 at full detail lived its first year in 53 s, and a founding spring of 200 cost 248 ms a day against 64 for 40. `civ-sim`'s `bench_village` example times a band of any size day by day, and a profile (callgrind) found where the time went (research 01-02 §2.3: stop unnecessary work, then keep hot paths compact). Four changes, each exact: the cells where new ground could be broken are listed once for each home's walking times (they were gathered and sorted again for each household each day of the spring, 70 % of the time); what a household knows of the land is put into one map per decision, keyed by resource and place, with a fast hasher for the engine's own integer keys (`civ_core::FastMap`), instead of a map per gathering activity with the default hasher; whom to ask for food, what to buy and where to work are worked out only when an option needs them, and then once; and route searches keep their scores in flat arrays reused between searches instead of a map (research 01-08 §7). Every section of a save was the same, byte for byte, after 30 days of a founding of 200 and after 20 days from its mid-May, with and without the changes. A founding spring of 200 now costs 85 ms a day, the first year 39 s, and a founding of 400 about 215 ms a day over its first three months: about in proportion to the people. Two tests read the first sickle sold, or the wage as posted, where another household could also buy, or a weekly review set the wage again; since runs differ by world they failed now and then, and now read the trade and wage they mean.
- **Larger foundings and families sent together (2026-10-04, M3a slice L):** a village of 200–500 needs more people than a band of 30–50 brings. A new world's founding band may now have up to 125 people, about half the largest Hutterite colony at its fission (research 05-06 §1.3: colonies split at 104–251 people; the report's sanity check starts a daughter settlement at half its parent's size). A larger band's homes stand farther from the hearth, by the square root of its number of families over eight (a default band's), so its camp is about as dense (a tuning value; a band of eight families or fewer camps exactly as before). The observer's god tool sends 1, 5, 10 or 20 families together (the host refuses more than 20), the daughter-coalition sizes of research 05-06 §5.4 (10 households, tested at 5–20), used here for a group that follows those who went before (chain migration, 05-06 §1.2). The first comes where the observer clicked, as a lone family always has; the others settle on dry ground on a spiral around it, 15 m apart (a tuning value), each drawn on its own and provisioned as a founding family is, and all join the settlement the first joins or founds. The core names pack has 70 given names for each sex, up from 30, so a village of hundreds repeats fewer. A founding of 125 joined by 40 families (seed 2) made a village of 302 in 67 households; over 120 days it went from 301 to 305 people, at 235 ms a simulated day. Wire 1.12 adds `SpawnFamily.families` (0 or absent means one); nothing is saved that was not before.
- **House size by wealth (2026-10-04, M3a slice L, research 10-06 §1.2, §2.3, §5.4; 08-14 §2.3, §3.7):** a hut is designed for the floor its household's members need (6 m² and 4.8 m² a resident, 10-06 §2.3, as before) and made larger by what the household puts into it from its means, 10-06 §2.3's status term. Its means are the goods it could spare, as it would offer them for sale, beyond what it keeps (food beyond what sees it to its next harvest, tools beyond its members' work, and materials once its home is finished; never seed, firewood or a fixed tool), valued in hours of its own work: with goods in store it need not work for, it can give more time to building. It puts in 0.3 of them, the middle of the 0.10, 0.30 and 0.60 that 08-14 §3.7 proposes for the share of discretionary resources offered to feasible investments, after needs and reserves (§2.3); a tuning value. The extra buys the hours of a larger hut, a decimetre of radius at a time, its materials' cutting and carrying included. Founders' provisions neither count nor cover what is kept: they carry a band to dependable harvests (05-06 §5.4). A hut takes no more than half the building time its household can give before its roof deadline (a tuning value), and no more than the program's largest, 4 m of radius and about 50 m², the top of Glastonbury's roundhouses (10-06 §2.1). A household that has a home builds a new one only when its means pay for all of one (all of it is beyond its needs) at least a quarter larger in floor area (a tuning value; 10-06 §5.4 has households extend and rebuild, and gives no threshold), as large as its members need: it builds beside the old, lives there until the new roof is on, then moves in and the old hut comes down. Wealth buys floor area alone, since the hut is one round room; 10-06 §1.2 finds wealth buying common and reception space more than sleeping room, which needs grammar v2 (M3b). Rafters are priced at one length, so the largest huts are a little cheap to build. A household checks its means once a day; at 238 ms a day against 235 for the village of 302 above, the cost does not show.
- **NUDGE: house size by wealth is silent so far (2026-10-04, M3a slice L):** in six-year runs of four seeds under both regimes (40-person bands), the floor-area Gini and the floor per house stayed within the spread of runs without the rule: 0.12–0.22 and 26–31 m² at the sixth year's end, against 0.15–0.22 and 25–29 m². Seed 2's households held goods worth 700–2,900 hours of their work in its fifth and sixth years, 70–1,740 of them to spare. A hut costs 450 hours for two to 1,050 for the largest (703 for five), cutting and carrying included, and the least a household in a hut of 26 m² would build again costs 780; so 0.3 of the most anyone could spare, about 520 hours, paid for no new home, and the few new couples brought little to spare to their first. House sizes follow household size at building. More surplus (better yields, livestock), huts that age and must be rebuilt (M3b's age and upkeep), or second buildings would give wealth occasions to show; none is forced here.
- **The morning's water (2026-10-04, found by the smoke):** with slice L's changes the 30-day smoke failed one river-valley world in about 35 runs: a household had no water at 06:00. Traced in a world that repeated it (1 in 17 tries): a household of four fetched 60 litres at noon, about 18 hours' worth, which ran out at 05:58; a member went for water after the morning meal, at 05:55, and was back at 06:01. The dusk rule above held that water lasting until sunrise lasted the night. Water at home must now last until sunrise and then through the first meal and the trip to the water; a household short of that fetches before dark. After the change no household went dry in 40 tries of that world, and 50 smoke worlds passed in a row.
- **Grain carried in store (2026-10-04, M3a slice L, research 08-05 §1.6; ADR-0006 §4):** the smoke's new price check found grain asked the same before the harvest as after: an ask moves from a cost anchor, and grain's own cost was the work of growing it whatever the season. A kilogram held in spring had to be grown as more than a kilogram the autumn before, since the store takes its share (08-05 §1.6: storage links harvest prices to later ones). A grown good's own cost is now its growing cost carried from the day its crop starts to ripen to the day it is reckoned, at its half-life under the household's roof or in the open: grain is cheapest as it ripens and dearest just before. Offers, purchases and the wealth measures' prices all use it. Under a roof (5 % a year) asks came out about 4 log points higher before the harvest than after, in every world of the ten-year smoke; the research's markets show 17–61 (16-01 §2.2), with the credit costs, risk and scarcity premiums the model does not have. ADR-0006 §4's other term, a review that also moves from the seller's stock against expected sales, is still not implemented. Two firm tests assumed the first workshop opened was the one they set up, and that a workshop faked idle could not sell again before its review; with other households now opening workshops too they failed now and then, and they now follow their own workshop and fake its idleness just before each night's reviews.
- **Smoke checks of the village economy (2026-10-04, M3a slice L, research 16-01, 08-14 §3.1, §3.2):** the smoke worlds take the content's property regimes in turn by seed. The ten-year run samples each settlement at the start of each month (the food its households hold, and what each seller asks for the crop's grain), and checks at each year's end that land is held and worked as the regime says (`claims_problems`: holders that exist and are of the regime's kind, leases only where it lets land and from a household to another, and under holders no field worked by another household without a lease), that no field was lost or moved, and that the latest wealth measures are numbers in their ranges that fit the regime; each of these fails a world. At the end it grades: **stocks**, whether food held rose from just before the crop ripens to just after the settlement's harvest is in, in each year after its first (red only if never); **asks**, the mean gap between the grain asked before and after (green above 2 log points; gray with fewer than two harvests to compare); **inequality**, the Gini of goods from the fifth year (amber below 0.3); and **workshops**, whether the hours worked for each over its life are right-skewed, the mean above the median (gray with fewer than ten). §4.7's band of 0.3–0.75 for the wealth Gini, a failure there, belongs to M3c's dashboard of 50-year runs: early farming need not show its inequality within ten years (08-14 §3.2: substantial variation, no immediate jump), so here a lower Gini is amber, reported and not hidden. The first ten-year run (all ten worlds passed, 10 min 41 s): stocks and asks green in all ten; inequality amber in all ten, Gini of goods 0.04–0.26 by the end; workshops green in four, amber in two (mean equal to the median), gray in four. Seven of the ten bands lived on; the bands of seed 1 failed in both landscapes, as before, and that of the coast's seed 4 died out in its tenth year.
- **The M3a demo (2026-10-04, M3a slice L, plan §7):** seed 2 (river valley, 6 km map), lived from the command line (`civ-host run`, which now reports each year's prices too, and with `--band` and `--families` makes a village of hundreds) under household fields and under village fields, three runs of each since runs differ; no seed was picked. **A band of 40, ten years:** at the tenth year's end, under household fields and village fields: 48–51 and 47–49 people, 12 households each; Gini of goods 0.15–0.19 and 0.16–0.18, of land worked 0.09–0.12 and 0.10, of land held 0.09–0.12 and 0 (the village held all 18–18.5 ha); floor area 0.21–0.22 in both and 27–28 m² a house; the top tenth 15–18% and 15–17% of the goods; 540–560 and 552–574 hours of goods a head; 0–1 field let and none (the regime lets none); a sickle sold for 3.6–3.9 and 3.6–4.4 hours of work, a hoe 2.3–2.5 and 2.5; grain asked at 1.87 in all as the year turned; no money anywhere; 8–24 and 15–28 workshops given up over the years. **A village of hundreds** (a band of 125 joined by 20 families, 214 people at the founding): at its fifth year's end 245–250 and 246–254 people in 59 households, with the same Ginis of goods (0.14–0.15 and 0.15), floor area (0.18) and floor per house (26 m²) under both; under household fields three fields were let for 99–155 kg of grain in rent. In its sixth year the harvest came in 37–41% short of the year before (35–38 t against about 60) and hunger followed under both. Under village fields 82–105 people died over the year and 5–7 left, leaving 154–175 at its end, with a Gini of goods of 0.27–0.32. Under household fields 25–73 died, but households left sooner (0, 129 and 229 people), leaving 189, 90 and 12, with Ginis of 0.11–0.24, and up to 38 fields let. Households left sooner under household fields and died more under village fields; why has not been traced. In the seventh year every household of every run left the valley.
- **NUDGE: the regimes part only under scarcity (2026-10-04, M3a slice L):** with land plentiful, households under either regime work what they need, and nothing else in the economy depends on who holds the ground; the regimes came out the same but for who holds land and the few fields let (research 08-14 §2.2: where households can get an independent livelihood, holders have little hold over them). Only a famine parted them. A village crowded onto too little land for long enough, with people who stay, would test whether tenure shapes wealth; that needs villages that survive scarcity (next NUDGE). Nothing was tuned to make them part.
- **NUDGE: a village of hundreds made at once does not last (2026-10-04, M3a slice L):** a band of 125 joined by 20 families never fed itself. Ground is broken only in the spring window, at 1,000 hours a hectare for new ground (plus 1,000 for woodland), so its fields grew from 35 to 81 ha in five years while its households needed about 100 (0.40 ha a head for a year's food at a cautious yield, 10-01 §2.3); it worked 0.31 ha a head and lived on what it carried and one good year, holding about four months' grain going into its sixth. Adults spent some ten hours a day resting or at the hearth, so labour was not short. A band of 40 has the same shortfall, but the valley's wild food covers far more of it. Likely remedies, for M3c's farming: breaking ground outside the sowing season, as farmers clear in autumn and winter, and reserves for more than the coming year (08-14 §3.7 proposes 30–180 days of reserve to test). Villages of 200–500 run at full detail (slice L's speed), but not for long.
- **M3b design (2026-10-04, plan §7):** four research briefs (knowledge and technique; buildings and grammar v2; structural rules, failure and upkeep; style, earthworks and deposits) from about 40 reports, their key citations checked against the reports. Six slices, M to R (§7). Three ADRs, the share §9's *M3 in three parts* set aside for M3b: [ADR-0008](decisions/0008-knowledge-carried-by-people.md) (knowledge carried by people), [ADR-0009](decisions/0009-building-components.md) (grammar v2, condition and trust) and [ADR-0010](decisions/0010-ground-people-change.md) (deposits and earthworks). Choices that depart from the plan's first sketch: discovery is a hazard on a session's qualified hours, drawn at its end and tied to the person working (07-01 §5.3, §5.5), not the yearly roll from population and specialists of §4.4 and §5.6 (07-01 §1.4 warns against population multipliers); a building's margin is kept per component group and decays with exposure, not one margin per building decaying with age (§5.1; 11-06 §5.1, §5.7); and technique trust is kept per settlement, which stands in for its culture until cultures exist (M5). Style needs no ADR: its traits are realised decisions in the spec's parameters, and taste is behaviour state.
- **Knowledge carried by people, as built (2026-10-04, M3b slice M, ADR-0008):** content API 9 adds the `technique` kind (a name, what a competent person can do, its skill, prerequisites as alternative routes, what discovery will need, `learn_h` and `upbringing`). Activities, recipes and building programs name the technique they need, and an activity that works a recipe needs the recipe's. The people profile's `[knowledge]` table gives the founders' shares, learners per teacher and the learning term. The validator adds E3005 (a technique gates nothing), E3006 (prerequisites in a cycle) and E3007 (a recipe that can never be worked because an input or tool comes only from recipes that need it: 07-03 §6's bootstrap test, as a fixpoint over the goods the world renews). Saves schema 13 keeps each person's entries in `People` (by technique id) and each settlement's record in a new `know` section; saves before it load with everyone given what a founder of their age would bring, and the records begin at the load. Wire 1.13 adds the techniques to `Welcome`, a person's entries to `PersonInfo`, `knowledge_rev` to the snapshot, the `GetKnowledge` query and the `IntroduceTechnique` command. Appended, never renumbered: four chronicle kinds (found, learnt, lost, introduced) and two decision reasons (*learning it from someone who knows*, and *does not know how, and nobody to learn from is at it*).
- **The founders' repertoire (2026-10-04, slice M):** eight techniques gate today's work: growing emmer, grinding at a quern, pounding grain, baking flatbread, knapping sickle blades, shaping stone, shaping wood and building roundhouses (research 07-04, 07-05, 07-07). Every founder old enough for each one's work brings it (share 1.0; 06-08 §3: founders arrive with substantial practical knowledge), and all are learnt at home (06-08 §1.1). Hours that teach them, all tuning values within 07-02 §2.3's 20–200 for a narrow procedure: 20 for baking, pounding and grinding, 100 for growing emmer and shaping wood, 200 for knapping, shaping stone and building roundhouses. A teacher takes two learners at once (07-02 §2.3: one to three), and learning beside someone adds 2 utility points to the work (about what useful work is worth; a tuning value).
- **Upbringing at the work's age (2026-10-04, slice M, ADR-0008 §4):** a child in a household where someone knows a technique learns it on reaching the youngest age of the work it gates: at their first such work or at the next day's start, so the gate never holds a child back from work their household would have them do. The window closes when they could keep a household (15), and work first done later (making an axe or a quern, from 16) is learnt in the year before: nobody leaves home without their household's work. The year is a tuning value. The first version closed the window at 15 for every technique, so nobody born in a world came to know shaping stone. The ten-year smoke's new knowledge check caught it: in each of the first four worlds to finish, some of those old enough for it did not know it (22 of 31 in one village's tenth year). A second version opened a year's window at the work's age (16 to 17). With it, all ten worlds passed the knowledge check, but one coastal world failed the roof check, 7 of 9 households roofed against 80%. One run cannot say whether gating played a part, since runs differ. But it left a known gap: someone keeping a household from 15, an orphan heading it say, would never learn shaping stone. The third version closes the gap.
- **What a settlement knows is derived when asked (2026-10-04, slice M, ADR-0008 §2, §5):** nothing is stored per settlement but its record of when each technique came and went. A death or departure looks over the settlement's residents for each technique the person knew, and the knowledge query derives each settlement's knowers, learners and those who have heard of a technique the same way. In villages of hundreds this costs nothing that shows; an index from technique to knowers waits until it does. ADR-0008 is amended to say so.
- **Slice M changes no behaviour, exactly (2026-10-04):** with the core content every founder knows everything and children learn it all at home, so gating work by knowledge must leave every world's life as it was. A test saves a band, loads the save with the core content and with a copy in which no work names a technique, and lives 120 days in both: everyone does the same things in the same places, the chronicle tells the same story but for knowledge, and every saved section but people's knowledge and the content is byte for byte the same. A child reaches the age of crop work half a day after the save. Removing the upbringing at the first work and the daily upbringing makes it fail. An ignored variant lives ten years (`cargo test -p civ-sim --test knowledge -- --ignored`, about seven minutes): it passed, and at its end everyone old enough for each technique's work knew it.
- **Slice M's review (2026-10-04):** a review of the slice found no critical defect and five smaller ones, all silent with the core content, where everyone knows everything.
  - **Fixed:**
    - A household's own cost of a good now takes each recipe at the skill of the best member who knows it, not of anyone (ADR-0008's consequence).
    - A teacher's two learners now count those on their way to the work.
    - A technique lost when a household leaves now counts the buildings it leaves standing as made with it.
    - A faster way of a farm task that the person does not know no longer rules out a slower one they do.
    - Content may hold at most 65,535 techniques, the 16-bit index people and saves use.
  - **Kept as a simplification:** a session of learning begun beside a teacher counts whole, though the teacher may finish first. Presence is checked when the learner chooses the work. With half of founders knowing each craft, 76 % of learning sessions ended after the teacher's.
- **Slice M in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** with the third upbringing rule, before the review's fixes, all ten worlds passed, the new knowledge check included. 8 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people: on the coast 18, 45, 28 and 57, and in the river valley 57, 20, 45 and 55. The bands of seed 1 fell to 3 and 0, as in every run since M1. A run of the build before slice M, made alongside, also passed all ten and kept 7: on the coast 15, 44 and 60, and in the river valley 52, 36, 46 and 57. One run each, and runs differ.
- **Discovery, as built (2026-10-04, M3b slice N, ADR-0008 §3):** at the end of each work session, each technique the session can find is a draw. A session finds a technique its activity counts toward (`tried_in`), or the one a session of trying was aimed at. The person must not know it, must know one of its routes of prerequisites wholly, and must have the goods it needs at home (the gate G). The draw is keyed by world seed, person, technique and the session's start, against 1 − exp(−ln 2 · qualified hours / E50) (07-01 §5.3). Qualified hours are 1 % of a routine session's hours (`experiment_share`, 07-01 §2.3) and all of a session of trying, three times for someone already aware of the technique (`aware_try_factor`, 07-02 §1.7's way back; a tuning value). The finder knows it at once. The chronicle names the kind of find: the first anyone made, the first here of what is known elsewhere, of what others here already knew (an independent find), or of what was lost here before. The settlement's record notes it if nobody there knew it. Content API 10, saves schema 14 (when each person last tried, and a session's technique), no wire change: the new decision reasons and the `try` behaviour reach the observer through the welcome's tables.
- **Trying, as built (2026-10-04, slice N):** a new behaviour, `try`, spends an hour at home toward the technique whose problem would cost the household the most food. The candidates are the techniques the person does not know and could find. Its worth is `w_try` (2.0, a tuning value) times the problem's share: food at risk over food at risk plus the trip half-worth of a household's days (the measure gathering trips use). Problems under 5 % of that draw no attention (a tuning value: a problem nobody has gets none, ADR-0008 §3). A person tries at most once a week (`try_gap_days` 7). That is about 2 % of working time, inside 07-01 §2.3's tested 0–5 % and 07-11 §2.2's 0–10 % protected time.
  - **The one problem built: food that will spoil before it is eaten.** It is computed forward from what the household holds rather than looked back on, so it needs no new state. A stock S of a good with decay rate λ, eaten at the household's daily need c after the foods that keep less long, loses S − (c/λ)·ln(1 + λS/c).
  - **Waiting for a technique that needs them:** ADR-0008 §3's other measures, hours spent on a task and work waiting on a good.
- **Preserving (2026-10-04, slice N):** a recipe preserves when its single food input keeps at least four times less long than what it makes (a tuning value). Drying meat (3 days) into dried meat (180) qualifies, while grinding grain into flour does not. A household makes it as much as would otherwise spoil, by the same closed form, when that is at least half a day of one person's food (a tuning value). Its worth is the food kept, weighed as food made ready is (`w_food`, with the trip half-worth).
- **Drying and smoking (2026-10-04, slice N, 07-04 P03; 07-03 §3.5):** not in the founders' repertoire, so a world finds it or not. It is found from hunting and fishing (1 % of their hours) and from trying at spoiling meat and fish, with firewood at home. E50 is 2,000 qualified hours, a near technique within 07-01 §2.3's tested 10³–10⁵ (a tuning value). It is learnt in 20 hours beside a knower and at home by children. Drying takes water and adds no calories (07-04): lean meat at about 70 % water dried to about 25 % keeps 0.4 kg a kilogram at 3,750 kcal/kg, and fish keeps 0.25 kg at 2,400. Both keep with half-lives of 180 days, 365 under a roof. Labour is 0.2 h/kg of meat and 0.15 of fish, plus an hour's setting up and 4 kg of wood a session, up to 30 kg. These are tuning values; the research gives no rates, and the days of hanging need no work.
- **The rotary quern as an imported route (2026-10-04, slice N, 07-04 P09; 08-03 §2.1):** it needs shaping stone and shaping wood. Grinding at a saddle quern counts 1 % of its hours toward finding it, at an E50 of 100,000 hours, so a village grinding some 6,000 hours a year would take thousands of years: in practice it comes from elsewhere, as the observer's introduction. It grinds at 0.25 h/kg against the saddle quern's 0.5 (Iron Age rotary querns, 3–5 kg an hour: 0.20–0.33 h/kg). Making one takes 40 kg of stone, 2 kg of timber and 40 hours (tuning values).
- **Tools for known work only (2026-10-04, slice N):** a household wants a tool for the members old enough for work that needs it, counting only work whose technique someone in the household knows. A tool only unknown work needs is not wanted at all, so no household wants a rotary quern until one of its members knows how to use it. Founders' kits, and tools given to saves from before schema 9, cover the techniques the people profile gives founders.

- **Slice N in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds passed, the knowledge check included. 6 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people: on the coast 41 and 18, and in the river valley 52, 42, 46 and 57. Coast seed 1 found drying and smoking in its sixth year, the only find in the ten worlds; its band, like river-valley seed 1's, died out, as both seed-1 bands have in every run since M1. The coast band of seed 2 fell from 45 to 7 in its seventh year, its households leaving together (the pattern of the NUDGE on founding risk), and kept 5; the coast band of seed 4 died out in its tenth year, as in slices K and L. Slice M's run kept 8. The time spent trying cannot account for it: a probe of coast seeds 1 and 2 counted every person-hour over seven years, and trying took at most 0.03 % of them (about 90 hours a year among some 45 people). One run each, and runs differ, so this does not show that slice N weakens villages; the cause of the coast seed-2 failure was not examined beyond the probe.
- **Slice O in three steps (2026-10-04, ADR-0009):** the grammar brief's B1 to B3. First the vocabulary with no change in what households do: the frame grammar, programs that say what they are for, saves and the boundary. Then a home chosen among programs, lofts sized by what a household stores, and storage as a roofed capacity. Then storehouses and workshops as second buildings. B4, loads on the groups, goes with slice P.
- **The frame grammar, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's first step, ADR-0009 §1–3):** version 1 builds a length of equal bays at a constant width under a gabled roof (research 10-06 §1.2, §2.1; 11-01 §3.4). At each frame line a post stands on each long wall and runs through every storey to the plates; a tie beam joins their heads; rafters rise in pairs from the eaves to a ridge. A loft is joists across the width on the plates over the bays the spec names, holding goods only (11-01 §2.4). An upper storey's floor and a raised floor are joists across the width on rails along the walls, with cross beams at the frame lines; a raised floor's middle rail stands on floor posts, which halves its joists' span. Walls are wattle and daub between the posts, storey by storey; the door's panel is split either side of the doorway; the gables are woven and daubed. Its sixteen parameters are ADR-0009 §2's list. Timber is member volume × 760 kg/m³ (11-08 §2.1), so a deeper joist costs real wood, and the roof is its projection over cos θ (11-04 §2.3). A spec is refused when it is off the 25 cm quantum, its length does not divide into whole bays, a value is outside the program's range, its door misses its wall, it names joists it lacks, it would need more than 32 groups (11-06 §5.1), a raised floor leaves no room for its posts, or the ladder to a raised door would stand beyond the roof's edge. Part and group ids are level, bay or frame line, kind and index, so a design with one more bay keeps every id it had (11-03 §4.8). Four goldens pin it (three bays with a one-bay loft, two storeys, a raised store, a workshop); the hut's golden is unchanged, because the hash covers the new fields only for frames, and a hut's five groups, its living space and its storage are derived beside its version-1 expansion.
- **Frame programs and their figures (2026-10-04, slice O's first step):** content API 11 has every building program say what it is for (`use`: dwelling, store or work), and a frame program's rules sit in a `[frame]` table with four material slots (timber, wattle, covering, boards). The core content has a **longhouse** (a dwelling of 1–8 bays of 2–4 m, 4–7 m wide, one or two storeys, lofts), a **granary** (a store of 1–2 bays, 2–4 m wide, its floor raised 0.5–1.2 m, after the raised, ventilated granaries of early farmers, 08-11 §2.1) and a **workshop** (a working floor of 1–4 bays with a store loft). The research gives ranges for bays, widths, storeys, eaves and pitches (10-06, 11-01, 11-05); the labour rates are the hut's where they are the same work, and a jointed frame's posts, beams and rafters are set so a three-bay house of 7.5 by 5 m takes about 1.1 pd8 a square metre of footprint for its frame, inside 11-04 §2.2's 0.5–2. That house with a loft over one bay takes about 940 hours, 2.1 t of timber and boards and 2.2 t of thatch (2.2 t is the brief's figure for its 72 m² of roof): about half as much again per square metre of living floor as a hut for five. Storage capacities are tuning values: 500 kg a square metre on a store's floor (grain about 0.65 m deep at 770 kg/m³, 11-05 §2.4), 150 in a loft, and 60 on living or working floor, which is also the hut's (`store_kg_per_m2`): room for a year's grain in a hut for five (1.26 t on about 30 m²). The second step raised living floor's figure to 100 (see storage as room, below). Places to work take 6 m² each (the research gives no figure).
- **Jointed timber framing (2026-10-04, slice O's first step, 07-07 WO02):** fitted joinery needs shaping wood and fitting skill; central European wells of 5469–5098 BCE were jointed with stone tools. The frame programs need it. Nobody knows it and nothing finds it yet (no work tries toward it, and it answers no problem), so a world has it only from the observer's introduction. It is learnt in 300 hours beside a knower and at home at building age (07-02 §2.3, a tuning value).
- **Households still build huts (2026-10-04, slice O's first step):** until households choose among programs, the people profile's home program must be a hut dwelling (a content error otherwise), and nothing a household decides reads the new vocabulary. One thing does change: a technique added to the content shifts the indexes of those after it, and finds are keyed by technique index, so finds come at other moments in a given world. A home a household holds that is a frame building (none can be, yet) counts as a round one with the same ground floor when it weighs building again, and wealth counts only dwellings' floor.
- **Saves 15, wire 1.14 (2026-10-04, slice O's first step):** saves schema 15 adds the Rect footprint (`length_cm`, `width_cm`, `angle`), sixteen parameters a design, and plot uses for stores and workshops; schema-14 saves load with their huts as they were, their other eight parameters 0. Wire 1.14 appends to `BuildingInfo` the grammar, the program's use, the size and direction, storeys, bays and loft bays, a gabled roof's corners and ridge, the apex height, floor by use, storage by kind and places to work. The observer draws a frame building's turned walls, posts and gabled roof with its ridge, and finds it under its roof rather than its circle.
- **The introduced quern's test after the founding rush (2026-10-04):** slice N's test of a rotary quern introduced on a world's first day failed in about one run in eight, since runs differ: in a world's first weeks the eldest can spend every day on fields and the first huts. That is what they would do, so the test now introduces it after 60 days, when every probe run made one within four days.
- **Homes chosen among programs, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's second step, ADR-0009 §1):** content API 12 turns the people profile's `home_program` into `programs`, the dwellings a household may build. A household builds one that someone in it knows how to build (the profile must include one its founders know). Each program's shapes are expanded once, when the content is compiled: a hut of each radius a decimetre apart, and a frame building of each count of bays and storeys, with lofts over none to all of its bays, to its usual sizes (a `[design]` table: 2.5 by 5 m bays, 15 cm posts, joists and walls, 50 cm eaves, tuning values). A first home is the shape that covers the household's members (10-06 §2.3's 6 m² plus 4.8 a resident) and its goods for the fewest hours of work, materials' carrying included. When none holds all its goods, it is the one covering its members that holds the most; when none covers its members, the one with the most living floor. Its means then buy the shape of the same program with the most floor that they pay for, as huts were sized before, so more means never give less floor. A household with a home builds again when its means pay for all of a home with at least a quarter more floor, in any program it may build. Its goods' room is what it holds now or a year's food as grain, whichever is more. A frame home's door is in the middle of the long wall that faces the hearth, its lofts from the back. Huts stay the cheaper home for five, as 11-04 §2.2's rates imply, so founders who know only huts build huts as before. The observer's introduction of a technique has its household plan its home again that day.
- **Storage as room under a roof, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's second step, ADR-0009 §5):** a household's roofed buildings give room by kind: a floor's once the roof is on, a loft's and a raised floor's once the building is finished. Goods with a sheltered half-life take the room, those that lose the most a day to the open per kilogram first (a fractional knapsack), raised floors first, then other room; the rest keep at the open rate. The shares are worked out from the stores as last settled, so spoilage is exact while the room holds everything (identical to before) and exact between settlings otherwise. The room is derived, never saved: at loading, when a roof goes on or a building is finished, when an old home comes down and when heirs take buildings, with stores settled first. Goods keep twice as long on a raised store's floor (`raised_store_factor`, a tuning value: grain's 5 % a year becomes 2.5 %, inside the 2–8 % a maintained dry store loses in 6–12 months, 08-02 §7.1). A hut's floor and a frame's living floor hold 100 kg a square metre. That is a tuning value, chosen by probing two ten-year smoke worlds (river valley seed 2 for five years, coast seed 3 for two): their households held at most 83 kg of such goods per square metre of hut floor (p90 37–70), so storage seldom binds in today's villages and M3a's calibration holds. A loft holds 150 kg/m², a store room's floor 500 (grain about 0.65 m deep).
- **Wire 1.15 (2026-10-04, slice O's second step):** `BuildingInfo` gains what its household keeps in it now, by kind of room, and in words: "loft over 1 bay: 1.2 t of 1.9 t, mostly grain". Each kind of room's goods are shared among a household's buildings by the room each gives.
- **Slice O's first step, after review (2026-10-04):** a review of the frame grammar found no blocking defects; these are its fixes. The grammar checks a floor's raise against its program's range whatever it is, so a program whose every floor is raised (the granary, 50–120 cm) refuses one on the ground; before, a raise of 0 slipped past the check. The granary's design is raised 80 cm (`[design] floor_raise_cm`, a tuning value; 08-11 §2.1), and its overhang now starts at 60 cm, so that the ladder to any raise its range allows stands under the roof. Content refuses a frame program whose bay or width could be no longer than its thickest post, and a program whose design gives nothing the grammar allows (E3001, with the grammar's reason). A household's housing counts only its dwellings: whether it is housed (and so may spend what it builds with, and stays where it lives while it builds) and how far its home is built; stores and workshops will give room for goods without housing anyone. (This step also made whether a household's stores are under a roof count dwellings only; that was wrong, since a store's roof shelters goods, and the third step undid it.) The map draws each building from a list of marks that a pure function works out (the plot, a gabled roof and its ridge or a hut's cone, the walls and posts going up, the doorway), and unit tests check them; the renderer only draws the marks. `expand_frame` is split into its layout, foundation, frame, walls, roof, finish, facts, needs and groups, with the goldens unchanged.
- **Slice O's second step in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds passed, the knowledge check included, and every good was accounted for in every year. 7 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people: on the coast 20, 48, 11 and 58, and in the river valley 55, 43 and 51. The bands of seed 1 fell to 3 and 0, as in every run since M1, and river-valley seed 3 fell to 3 in its fifth year and kept 4. Drying and smoking were found in three worlds: coast seed 2 (twice) and river-valley seed 2 in their seventh year, and river-valley seed 1 in its sixth. Nobody in these worlds knows jointed framing, so every home was a hut; what changed for them is storage as room under a roof, which seldom binds, and a hut sized for goods as well as members when its household holds more than its floor would. Slice N's run kept 6. One run each, and runs differ. The economy grades: food stocks and grain asks green in all ten worlds, inequality green in one and amber in nine, workshops green in three and too few to judge in seven.
- **Storehouses, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's third step, ADR-0009 §7):** content API 13 lets the people profile's `programs` name stores and workshops beside dwellings (at least one a dwelling its founders know how to build); the core profile adds the granary. A household builds one thing at a time: what it has under way (a home first), else its first home, else a storehouse if one is worth it, else a new home if its means pay for all of one (B2's rule). A storehouse is worth it when what the goods its roofs have no room for would lose in the open over a horizon, less what they would lose on its floors (raised floors keep twice as long), valued at the household's own cost of each good, exceeds the hours it takes, materials' carrying included; among the shapes its means pay for in full (as for a new home) and its time allows, the one that saves the most beyond its cost. The horizon is three years (`store_horizon_days`, a tuning value: grass thatch wants major repair every two to three years, 11-08). A granary stands within 30 m of its home, its door toward it, on a plot of its own (use `store`), and never moves its household's home; its roof is wanted by 1 September. At the core content's figures a one-bay granary takes about 530 hours and holds 3.75 t raised, two bays about 890 hours and 7.5 t; grain's own cost was about 1.6 hours a kilogram in the tests' world, so a tonne beyond the roof saves too little, eight tonnes are worth two bays, and seed kept for sowing, which is never spare, gives no means to build one. While a household builds anything, what it builds with stays for that (before, only until its home was finished). Nobody knows jointed framing in an ordinary world, so storehouses come only with the observer's introduction and play no part in the ten-year smoke.
- **A store before a larger home (2026-10-04, slice O's third step):** a hut's floor holds goods at 100 kg/m² and costs about 23 hours a square metre, so a household whose spare grain fills its hut always has the means for a home a quarter larger before it could want a store: the first version, which looked for a new home first, built longhouses where the test looked for a granary. A household losing goods for want of room now protects them first, and spends what is left on floor. The new-home rule itself is unchanged.
- **Workshops, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's third step, ADR-0009 §7):** content API 14 adds `home_work_places` to the people profile's `[build]` (two, a tuning value; the research gives no figure for how many work at a craft in a home) and the workshop to the core programs. A firm counts, whenever a session of work for it ends, how many are at work for it at that moment, owners and hired hands, and remembers the most it has seen lately (`AT_ONCE_DAYS`, 60, a tuning value: long enough that a busy season is still remembered when the household next decides what to build). A household with a home and no store worth building builds a workshop for its busiest firm that has had more at work at once than a home holds and has no workshop: the cheapest work program's building with places for them all (6 m² a place) that its means pay for in full and its time allows, beside its home with its door toward it. A firm alone never begins one. The building names its firm (`Building.firm`). Once its roof is on, the firm works there: its owners walk there to make what it sells (with the walk weighed like any other), and the hands it hires walk there instead of to the owners' home; its stock is still kept, and sold, at its owners' home. When a firm closes its building stays, and the household's next firm works in it (see the review's fixes below for how it is handed on). Heirs take every building with the firms that pass to them; a household that is no more leaves its buildings standing. Nobody knows jointed framing in an ordinary world and hired work is rare, so workshops come only with the observer's introduction and play no part in the ten-year smoke. The tests set the count by hand: a firm that had three at work at once gets a two-bay workshop of five places (about 1,000 hours); one that had two gets none.
- **Saves 16, wire 1.16 (2026-10-04, slice O's third step):** saves schema 16 adds the firm a building is the workshop of, a firm's most at work at once and the day it was seen, and each settlement's yearly roofed floor and room for goods a household; schema-15 saves load with no building naming a firm, nobody counted at work and those measures 0, and a building naming a firm the save does not have is refused. Wire 1.16 appends to `BuildingInfo` its firm and the firm's name, and to the wealth measures each household's floor under all its roofs and room for goods, and those a household in each settlement's spread. House floor area stays homes only (ADR-0007 §4).
- **Fixes from the third step's review (2026-10-04, slice O):** a review found no blocking defect and four to fix, each with a test. (1) A building under way that nobody in its household can work on (its program left the content, or nobody in it knows its technique any more) no longer holds the household up: it waits, the household plans and builds other things, and what it builds with is kept only for buildings it can work on (it had pinned every material for good). (2) A household tries its projects in order, store, then a workshop for each firm that needs one (the busiest first), then a larger home, and begins the first it can pay for and find ground for; before, it committed to one kind and gave up if that failed, and a firm busier than any workshop has places for (above ten at the core figures) shadowed every other firm for two months. That firm now gets the largest workshop its means allow. A workshop planned in the morning is begun only for a firm still open and without one. (3) A firm works only in the building linked to it. A building whose firm closed goes to the household's oldest open firm that has none, when a firm is founded and each day; before, any firm without one used it, and a new firm then took it from them.
