A firm that outgrows its workshop stays in it: there are no extensions yet. (4) The map's building revision counts the firm link, so the observer hears when a workshop changes hands. The smoke now counts a household as under a roof only under its own home's, and checks the new wealth measures are numbers in range.
- **Slice O's ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, after its third step):** all ten worlds pass (`smoke --years 10 --seeds 5 --size 1024`). Six of ten villages keep ten or more people (coast 3: 41, coast 5: 19; river valley 2: 56, 3: 39, 4: 46, 5: 58); coast 2 ends at 8, and coast 1, coast 4 and river valley 1 die out (coast 1 after falling to 3 in year 8, the others in years 10 and 6). Its second step kept seven, inside the six to eight of earlier slices; as worlds draw differently with every change of content, one run cannot tell the two apart. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in four, amber in one and grey in five. No finds were noted. Nobody knows jointed framing in an ordinary world, so homes are huts, and the one change these worlds see is that materials are kept while any building is under way.
- **Slice P in three steps (2026-10-04, ADR-0009 §4–6):** first condition, wear and upkeep with no loads, so buildings age and are kept before anything can fail under a load; then loads, checks and failures with their chronicle; then caution per settlement and technique, with the demo test. Each step is usable and pushed on its own.
- **Building condition, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's first step, ADR-0009 §4, §6):** each component group of a building is put in place when the stage that builds it is finished, with a quality drawn once, keyed by the world, the building and the group: one less the spread at its builders' average skill times the size of a normal draw, never below 0.1. The spread runs from 0.30 for a novice (the one dispersion figure the research gives, a building-to-building log-dispersion, 11-06 §2.2) to 0.10 for a master (a tuning value), so novices' parts average 0.76 of what sound members of their sizes carry and masters' 0.92; skill buys fit and consistency, not stronger wood (11-05 §1.3, 11-08 §1.2). The builders' skill is the building skill (`core:skill/building`, content API 15) of whoever did each hour of the stage, averaged over its hours; building and mending train it (1,500 hours to 80 % of mastery, founders 0.2–0.7: tuning values, no report gives a builder's apprenticeship). Skill does not change how fast anyone builds. Quality is kept and shown, and does nothing yet: loads and margins come with the next step.
- **Wear, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's first step):** on the first of each month every group in place loses a twelfth of its kind's yearly share (each program's `[upkeep]`): a covering in the weather, infill at the wall's foot, posts at their foot by the wetness of the habitat the building stands in (the land profile's `wetness`: open water and wet margins 2, floodplain 1.3, woodland 1, upland 0.7, tuning values), and roofed timber only by how much the roof over it leaks. A group shows once its loss passes its kind's threshold and fails once it is all lost. At the core figures thatch loses 6 % a year and leaks from 15 %, so it shows after two and a half years, when the research wants significant mending (every 2–3 years, 11-08 §2.4), and is gone in about 17 untended. Posts lose 2 % a year at wetness 1 and show at 30 %; daub 5 % and shows at 20 %; roofed timber 10 % a year under a full leak (all tuning values: no report gives rates for posts in the ground or daub at a wall's foot, 11-06 §2.2, 11-08 §2.4). Decay is by exposure, never by calendar age (ADR-0009, forbidden list). A roof leaks by how far its coverings have gone beyond the share at which they leak, averaged over them, and fully once its frame or a covering has failed; the room under it for goods shrinks by that share, so what no longer fits keeps as in the open (a tuning choice: the research gives no figure for what a leak costs goods). Failed loft joists drop that loft's room. Posts that fail leave a ruin, which is no roof and no home: its household builds anew and takes the ruin down when the new roof is on; any other failure leaves the building damaged. Nothing yet fails under a load, and nobody is hurt.
- **Upkeep, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's first step):** a household mends before it builds anything new, after what it has under way: the repair under way, else the group gone furthest beyond the share at which it shows, of all its standing buildings. A repair renews what is lost of that group with that share of the group's part of its stage's labour and materials (the group's part by its members' volume, or its area for a covering or infill), and changes only that group (11-08: a new roof protects a wall without restoring what it lost; 11-06 §5.7). Its quality stays. It is weighed like building, wanted by the same roof deadline, and its materials are fetched as for building; so it is deferred while other work presses, as houses stay lived in while they show distress (11-06 §1.3). In a test a hut whose thatch had lost 60 % was mended within the season with thatch at hand: about 80 hours of thatching, begun within a week.
- **Founder skills drawn on their own (2026-10-04, slice P's first step):** a founder's skill levels are now drawn from a stream keyed by the world and the person, as their knowledge already was, so adding a skill to the content changes none of the other founding draws. The building skill first changed every band: in the seed the tests use for births, nobody arrived expecting. A skill the content has that a save never named is given to everyone on loading as a founder of their age would bring it.
- **Saves 17, wire 1.17 (2026-10-04, slice P's first step):** saves schema 17 adds each building's groups' quality, loss, state and when each was put in place and last mended, the building's state, its builders' skill hours on the stage under way, and the repair under way. Saves of schema 16 and earlier load with every group of a finished stage in place and sound as of loading, drawn as if built at middling skill (0.5), and the stage under way as worked at middling skill, so a loaded world shows no false wave of decay (ADR-0009 §8). Wire 1.17 appends to `BuildingInfo` its state, what it shows in words ("the thatch leaks; rot at the posts' foot"), the share of its roof that leaks, each group's condition and the upkeep under way in words ("mending the covering, 40% done"). The observer darkens a leaking roof and draws a ruin as bare posts.
- **Slice P's first step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds pass. Six of ten villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 21, 3: 42, 5: 25; river valley 2: 54, 4: 48, 5: 52); river valley 3 ends at 5 (down to 7 in year 4), and coast 1, coast 4 and river valley 1 die out. Slice O's third step also kept six. The economy checks: stocks green in nine and amber in one, asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in seven and grey in three. River valley 4 found drying and smoking in year 10. Each world ends with 9 to 13 buildings, all standing: no ruin and none damaged. The six villages that kept ten or more mended 16 to 25 parts in ten years (each counted once however often mended), and all but coast 2 (5 of 11 showing) end with nothing showing; river valley 3, down to five people, mended 8 and ends with 7 of 9 showing; in the villages that died out, every building shows its wear, with nobody left to mend it.
- **Loads and failure, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's second step, ADR-0009 §5):** each day every standing building is weighed against what it carries. Beams, joists and rafters are simply supported poles under a spread load: bending stress `W·L/8` over the section modulus `π·d³/32`, deflection `5·W·L³/(384·E·I)` (research 11-05 §5.2). Posts carry their share of the building's weight, its roof's and its lofts' and floors', against Euler's buckling load (halved for crooked poles and loose joints, a tuning value) or crushing, whichever is less. What a part carries is its covering's weight (from the grammar's materials), the goods its household keeps on it (the goods under a household's roofs, shared among its buildings by room), people on floors off the ground at 1.5 kPa (11-05 §2.4's occupied-dwelling scenario), and the month's peak of wind and snow on roofs. Timber is content (API 16, the good's `[timber]`): green oak's bending strength of 57 MPa, compression of 24.5 MPa and stiffness of 8.6 GPa (11-05 §2.3); creep φ = 1 (11-05 §2.4); and 0.6 of its strength under load carried for years (a tuning value, as the research gives no duration-of-load factor). A part's quality scales its strength, and its loss takes strength by `(1 − x)³` in bending and buckling strength by `(1 − x)⁴` (11-05 §5.1). A margin below 1 is a failure; below 1.5, or a sag past L/180, shows (tuning values). The research's L/180 is serviceability, not collapse (11-05 §2.4), so a sag is only a symptom, and failure needs a strength margin below 1 (ADR-0009 §5 read so). A loft or floor that gives way spills its goods, 30 % of them lost (a tuning value); a tie beam at the wall heads that breaks brings its lofts down; rafters that break leave the roof open to the sky; posts that give way leave a ruin. Upkeep rebuilds a part that gave way whole, but does not mend a sag or a lean, which only lighter loads cure. At the core figures, a full loft (150 kg/m²) on the longhouse's 15 cm poles over its 5 m width holds work of fair quality (a margin of about 2.7) but sags past L/180, as the brief's design note said it would; poles a novice chose badly (quality below about 0.3) give way under it; over 7 m even fair joists show the strain. A hut's twenty 12 cm posts carry its thatch and a 0.6 kPa peak about 39 times over, and give way once about three quarters rotted.
- **The month's peak load (2026-10-04, slice P's second step):** wind and snow on roofs are one draw a month for each settlement, log-normal with a median of 0.25 kPa and a spread of 0.6 (the land profile's `[peak_load]`, tuning values: wind at 20 m/s presses about 0.25 kPa and at 40 m/s about 1 kPa, 11-05 §2.4), so one month in twenty brings more than about 0.7 kPa and one in a thousand more than 1.6. It stands in for weather until M3c and will be replaced by it (ADR-0009 §5). The draw is evaluated once, never rerolled (11-06 §4); buildings were weighed each day under their month's peak, as if it came on its first day, which the step's smoke showed wrong: the storm now comes on one day of its month (see the smoke entry below).
- **NUDGE: who dies when a building gives way (2026-10-04):** the research gives no defensible share (11-06 §5.3: those on, below and beside the failed part are at risk). Each person inside, not on their way somewhere and within the building's reach, dies with a chance of 0.3 when its posts give way, 0.15 when its roof falls in and 0.05 when a loft or a floor gives way: tuning values, and the cause *collapse*. Buildings are weighed at the start of each day, when most people are at home asleep. Revisit with weather (M3c), when a storm's hour can be known.
- **Saves and the boundary for failures (2026-10-04, slice P's second step):** a person's cause of death gains *collapse* (saved as 3), and the chronicle a building that gave way (code 21): its household's eldest, those it killed, where, and in words what gave way, under what and why ("Wren's longhouse lost its loft: the joists broke under 3.8 t of provisions; they were poorly made"). Both are appended codes in fields saves already have, so the save schema stays 17 and older saves load as before. What a part shows of its load (the loft sags, the posts lean) is saved as its state and said in words on the wire; margins themselves are derived and never saved.
- **Slice P's second step, ten-year smoke, and its correction (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds passed every check, but buildings gave way far too often: 3 to 34 times a world and 245 in river valley 2, killing 1 to 15 people a world (80 deaths in all). The research's sanity band is 1e-5 to 1e-3 failures a building-year (11-06 §2.4); ten to thirteen buildings over ten years should see about one failure in ten worlds. A probe of river valley 2 found every failure in its first three years a hut's roof breaking "in a storm", four in one month. Two causes, both fixed. First, the month's peak struck every day of its month, so a roof mended after a storm met the same storm the next week: the draw now also picks the day it comes (1 to 28) and strikes only then, so each storm is evaluated once (11-06 §4, §5.2). Second, a hut's rafters are two thirds of its posts' diameter, and its 12 cm posts gave a big hut (3.4 to 3.6 m radius) 8 cm rafters over 5.5 to 5.8 m, whose margin was 1.0 at about 1 kPa when new and sound, a storm of about one month in a hundred. The hut's posts are now 15 cm and its rafters 10 cm (content: `post_diameter_cm`, `post_kg` 34, `rafter_kg` 33; tuning values, as no report sizes a hut's rafters), which carry a big hut's thatch through about 2 kPa when sound, so a roof that falls in is one poorly made or rotted under a leak. A hut for five now takes about 1.5 t of timber rather than 1 t. The step's other figures stand.
- **Caution, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's third step, ADR-0009 §6):** each settlement remembers, for each building technique, the failures of its buildings (one a building that gave way, and each death in it as two more: a tuning value) and the building-years its standing buildings of it have stood, counted on the first of each month. Both fade by half over 8 years (inside 11-09 §2.3's 2 to 20 for personal memory of a disaster, low confidence). Their ratio is the failure rate builders believe in, against at least 10 building-years so a first failure among new buildings is not taken for a rate of one in a few. Caution is `1 + (most − 1)·rate/(rate + half_rate)`, at most 2 and half way at 0.01 failures a building-year, ten times the top of 11-06 §2.4's band (tuning values; the people profile's `[build.caution]`, content API 17). A frame building is designed with its joists' diameter times the cube root of caution and its posts' times the fourth root (bending strength goes as d³, buckling as d⁴), to the nearest centimetre within the program's range, so twice the strength is 19 cm joists and 18 cm posts for the longhouse's 15 and 15. Years without failures bring caution back, since the years keep counting while the failures fade. A household chooses what to build by the usual design's cost and pays for the stronger one as it builds (the stage needs follow the design). Huts are built as always: their grammar is frozen and leaves nothing to size. A household of no settlement remembers nothing, and lessons are about a technique's buildings as a whole, not yet about one span, material or load (11-06 §5.6).
- **Saves 18, wire 1.18 (2026-10-04, slice P's third step):** saves schema 18 adds to the knowledge section what each settlement has seen of each technique's buildings: its failures and building-years and when they were last brought up to date. Saves of schema 17 and earlier load with no settlement having seen anything, so caution starts at 1. Wire 1.18 appends to `TechniqueHere` the caution there and its words ("built 1.9 times as strong: failures weigh 1.0 against 12 building-years"), shown in the knowledge panel.
- **Slice P's third step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, with the storm and hut-rafter corrections):** all ten worlds pass. No building gave way in any world, so nobody died of a collapse and caution stayed at 1 everywhere: inside the research's band (11-06 §2.4), where step two's smoke had 3 to 245 failures a world. Seven of ten villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 23, 3: 42, 4: 14, 5: 56; river valley 2: 58, 4: 48, 5: 50), one more than slice P's first step; coast 1 ends at 3 (down to 6 in year 7), river valley 3 at 5 (down to 5 in year 4), and river valley 1 died out in year 6. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality green in two and amber in eight, workshops green in five and grey in five. Villages mended 11 to 24 parts. Every building of every world ended showing something, where the first step's living villages ended with nothing showing: resolved in the next entry.
- **Roofs bow without showing (2026-10-04, after slice P's third step):** a probe of river valley 2 found that every building showed one thing only, its roof frame, with almost no rot: a hut's rafters under 30 kg/m² of thatch bend 1.2 to 2.5 times past L/180 over the years (creep φ = 1), so every roof "sagged" from the start and upkeep could never clear it. 11-05 §2.4 gives the L/180 to L/360 screen as a later engineering analogue for beams, not past practice, and thatched rafters bow without harm, so the sag is now checked only on lofts, floors and beams, where it hinders use. A roof frame shows only its strain (a margin below 1.5) and gives way below 1, as before. After 30 months the probe's 13 buildings show nothing. Its ten-year smoke (before deposits, which shift every world) passes in all ten worlds, and nothing gave way. Six villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 30, 3: 13, 5: 55; river valley 2: 51, 4: 46, 5: 54); coast 1 ends at 4 and river valley 3 at 7, and coast 4 (in year 10) and river valley 1 (in year 6) die out. The four largest end with nothing showing and coast 2 and 3 with 3 of 12 and 4 of 9; the dwindled and dead bands' buildings show their wear, with too few or nobody to mend them. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in four, amber in two and grey in four. Coast 2 and river valley 2 found drying and smoking in year 7.
- **Deposits as bodies, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's first step, ADR-0010 §1):** the land profile's `[[deposit]]` rules (content API 18) lay down bodies of a good once, from the seed, on land cells whose slope and height above drainage fall in each rule's ranges: a count about `per_km2` times the qualifying land (at most 256 a rule), each at a qualifying cell drawn at random, its radius, cover, thickness and quality drawn within ranges, showing at the surface when nothing covers it or by `exposed_share`, holding its volume times `density_kg_m3`. The research describes where clay, stone and flint lie only in words (03-05 §1.1-1.2), so every figure in the core profile is a tuning value: clay on flat floodplain 0.5-6 m above a channel, 4 bodies a qualifying km² of 10-40 m radius under up to 1.5 m of cover; stone on slopes over 0.15, 2 a km², 0.1-0.6 of it fit for use (03-05 §2.2), at 2,500 kg/m³ (11-08 §2.1); flint on upland slopes, 0.5 a km², 250 kg of nodules a cubic metre of bed (no figure in the research). A new good, clay, is what clay bodies yield. A deposit is a body with a permanent id; what is taken plus what is left is always what it began with. A new world places its deposits after its founding band arrives, so the band's founding draws are as they were; later ids shift, and with them every world. Slice H's loose stone and flint per patch stay as they were. Saves schema 19 keep the bodies and what has been taken from each in a `deposits` section; a save from before it gains, on loading, the bodies a new world of its seed has (with ids of its own). Nobody finds or digs a deposit yet.
- **Finding deposits and the observer's tool, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's first step):** when someone ends a walk, their settlement finds every body showing at the surface, with something left in it, within 50 m of the edge of the route they walked (a tuning value: the research gives no figure, and looking reveals indications rather than a body's outline, 03-05 §1.3). The settlement keeps who found it and when; the chronicle says so (code 22, appended). A buried body waits for an earthwork to cut into it; a household of no settlement keeps no record. The observer's tool lays down a body of any good on dry land, a disc of the radius asked (1 to 200 m), showing or under the cover the land profile's rule for the good gives, with the middle of that rule's thickness and quality and its density (a metre, 0.6 and 1,800 kg/m³ for a good it lays down none of); the chronicle records it (code 23). Saves schema 19 keep each settlement's finds beside the bodies. Wire 1.19 adds the deposits query and its revision in the snapshot (each deposit with its good, place, size, cover, quality, what is left and taken, and who found it in words) and the command to lay one down; the observer draws them on the map, describes them in the readout and has the tool beside *Add a family*.
- **Slice Q's first step, ten-year smoke, and a part rebuilt as new (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds pass. Each lays down 48 to 104 deposits (24 to 60 showing at the surface), and by year 10 villages have found 1 to 14 of them. Six keep ten or more people (coast 2: 15, 3: 38; river valley 2: 58, 3: 12, 4: 50, 5: 56); coast 1 ends at 2, and coast 4 and 5 (year 10) and river valley 1 (year 6) die out. Deposits shift every world's later ids, so these are other worlds than the last smoke's. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six and grey in four. But coast 2's buildings gave way 46 times, killing 6. A probe found every one a hut's roof whose rafters were poorly made, most under the thatch alone, the same roof again and again: mending kept a part's quality even when the part had given way and was rebuilt whole, so rafters too poor to carry their thatch were rebuilt just as poor. A part rebuilt after it gave way is now new members, its quality drawn afresh from its mender's building skill (keyed also by when, so each rebuilding is its own draw and no earlier draw moves); mending a worn part still keeps its quality, the new members fitted among the old. The probe's ten years of coast 2 then have 1 failure instead of 43.
- **The rebuilt part's ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** with a part that gave way made anew, all ten worlds pass, and one building gave way in all of them (coast 2's, killing 2), where coast 2 alone had 46. Five villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 37, 3: 41; river valley 2: 61, 4: 49, 5: 54), the least the check allows; coast 1 ends at 5, coast 5 at 8 and river valley 3 at 6, and coast 4 (in year 10) and river valley 1 (in year 6) die out. River valley 1's band has died out within ten years in every smoke since slice M but slice P's second step, which left 3, so it is not this change's doing; it wants a probe of its own. Villages mended 7 to 24 parts and found 5 to 16 of their 48 to 104 deposits. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six and grey in four.
- **Levelling plots, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's second step, ADR-0010 §2):** a household that builds a home, store or workshop seeks level ground first: of the clear plots within the 30 m it would move a building, ring by ring outward from its home, it takes the first whose ground drops no more than `from_m` (0.4 m) across it, and when there is none the one that drops least. It never takes one that drops more than `most_m` (2 m), nor one whose platform's sides could reach a cell of water (earthworks are refused on or beside water while routing and drainage stay as generated, ADR-0010 §3). These are the people profile's `[build.levelling]` (content API 19); the research gives no threshold (11-12 §2.5B notes only that buildings often adapt to slopes instead), so both are tuning values. The ground is the generated bed, its cells' heights joined bilinearly between their centres. A plot that must be levelled gets a platform: an earthwork record with its rectangle, its level in centimetres, its sides' run (1.5 across to 1 up: 11-12 §2.3's figure for canals in ordinary earth, a tuning value for a platform's low sides), its plot and household, the earth it cuts and the share done. Its level is the one at which all it cuts, sides and all, equals all it fills, so earth is moved within the ground and never made. Volumes balance as the earth lay in the ground, as if fill were tamped back to its density in the bank; 11-12 §2.4's 0.90 compacted-to-bank prior is not yet used. The earth costs 11 hours a cubic metre cut, added to the building's first stage and done with its first hours: 8 to dig it with wooden, antler and stone tools (11-12 §2.4's prior for a non-metal kit, 1.0 m³ an 8-hour day, low confidence), 2.4 to spread and tamp it as fill (3 m³ compacted a day) and half an hour to carry it across the plot in baskets (a tuning value). 11-04 §2.2's excavation experiments dig two to four times faster, with their tools unstated. A middling hut's 6 m plot that drops a metre across it cuts 6 to 7 m³ (A·drop/8 within it, 11-12 §2.5B, and its sides), about 70 hours.
- **The ground's changes, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's second step, ADR-0010 §3):** what earthworks have done to the ground is kept as each cell's change in mean height, in tiles of 64 × 64 cells with revisions, beside the generated bed, which is never rewritten. A platform's pure, versioned expansion (version 1) samples its plot and sides every half metre and adds each sample's change to the cell it falls in. Advancing a platform from one share done to the next adds only the difference, so no change is applied twice. A test expands the records afresh and finds the tiles, and a golden hash pins version 1. Where two platforms' sides overlap (plots keep a gap of only a metre), their changes add, each designed on the generated bed (a simplification). Walking, water and the map still read the generated ground: routing and drainage stay as generated in M3b (ADR-0010 §3), and the elevation the observer is served gains the changes with the boundary's next step.
- **Saves 20 (2026-10-04, slice Q's second step):** saves schema 20 add an `earth` section with each earthwork's record and the ground's changed tiles, each with its revision. Saves of schema 19 and earlier load with no earthworks and the ground as generated, so a building begun before then stands on its plot as it is.
- **Wire 1.20 (2026-10-04, slice Q's second step, ADR-0010 §4):** the snapshot gains an earthworks revision, which changes when an earthwork is begun or advanced or the building on its plot changes. A new query lists every earthwork with its rectangle, level, sides, earth and share done, its household, plot and building, and what it is in words ("the plot of Ada's hut, being levelled: 40% of 6.4 m³ cut and filled"), with every tile of the ground's changes and its revision. The elevation raster now serves the ground in use, the generated bed plus those changes; water, drainage and lakes stay as generated. The observer draws each platform as bare earth, more solid as more of it is done, describes it in the readout, and reads its map tiles again where a tile's revision changed. Levelling is rare: households find level ground first, and in a probe of sixteen 512-cell worlds over their first 30 days, only two coast worlds levelled any plots (one and four, all on the first day). The web shell now declares the wire minor it speaks, which had stayed at 1.16 since slice O.
- **Slice Q's second step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, at the levelling commit):** all ten worlds pass. Levelling is rare: coast 4 and river valley 1 levelled 2 plots each (7 m³) and coast 5 levelled 6 (15 m³); the other seven levelled none. Seeking level ground moves where every household builds, so these are other worlds than the last smoke's. Six villages keep ten or more people (coast 3: 14, 5: 57; river valley 2: 46, 3: 32, 4: 48, 5: 55), one more than the last; coast 2 ends at 4, and coast 1 (year 8), coast 4 (year 10) and river valley 1 die out. The coasts swung both ways (coast 2 from 37 to 4 and 3 from 41 to 14, coast 5 from 8 to 57), and the ten worlds hold 256 people against the last smoke's 261, so nothing here points to the change. One building gave way in all ten (coast 2's, killing 1). Villages mended 16 to 25 parts and found 4 to 17 deposits. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six and grey in four.
- **Pots as stores, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step):** a good's new purpose `store` (content API 20), with `[store] keeps_kg`, is a vessel food is kept in. A household counts each store it holds as turning that many kilograms of the room in its lofts and on its floors into raised room, so the food in it keeps at the raised floor's factor. That is a tuning choice: 07-05 §3.1 lists storage among fired pottery's uses, and the research gives no keeping figure for a pot. A store stands in the room it keeps, so stores add no room and food in the open gains nothing from them. A household under a roof wants as many stores as would hold the food lying in its lofts and on its floors. It makes them by the preserving term, the food the next one would save over the store horizon (three years) valued in kcal, as long as that is at least half a day of one person's need. A household with no roof wants none. Stores are offered for sale as materials are. The core pot is a large storage pot of about 45 litres, some 8 kg of fired clay, holding 35 kg, and half are gone in three years (tuning: the research gives no size or use-life).
- **Pottery, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step):** making pottery is a technique founders bring (07-05 §3.1: fired pottery among foragers 20,000 years ago, so it needs no farming before it; 06-08 §3), learnt at home, with a skill of its own taken as 1,500 hours to T80 like the others. A session forms up to five pots and fires them in an open fire in an hour (08-03 §2.1: open firings of one to three hours). Each pot is 6 hours of work (07-05 §2.3: 0.3-2 hours a kilogram of fired ware, taken at 0.75 for 8 kg), 10 kg of clay and 12 kg of firewood (0.5-3 kg of dry wood a kilogram, taken at 1.5), and one in ten breaks in the firing (08-03 §2.2's worked example). Drying between forming and firing (08-03 §2.1: a day, then three to seven more) is not kept apart: a session forms and fires its pots. A household short of the clay or firewood for the pots it wants brings it as it brings any material.
- **Digging, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step, ADR-0010 §2):** a new behavior, `dig` (content API 20), names the good it `digs`, and the land profile must lay down deposits of that good. People dig at the deposit of it their settlement knows that brings most for the walk, while their household needs the good for what it builds or makes, as a material is gathered. They dig at its open pit, or where a new one would go: the free 3 m square on the body nearest the settlement's hearth, its heap beside it. An hour of a capable adult's digging brings, on average over a pit's life, the body's density times its quality times its share of the pit's depth (thickness over cover plus thickness), over `h_per_m3`. The people profile's `[digging]` gives 8 hours a cubic metre, 11-12 §2.4's non-metal prior for digging and lifting (levelling's 11 adds tamping and carrying across the plot), and 3 m pits (tuning). A household weighs a pit by that average, so it begins one under deep cover as readily as one under none, and the first sessions there bring nothing home: a 3 m pit under 1.5 m of silt is 108 hours of digging before any clay. A session digs until its load (20 kg) is full or its hours are spent. The cover and the share of the body unfit for use go on the heap; what is taken of a deposit is kilograms of its body, of which its quality is carried home. A pit is dug through its body before another is begun on it. Plots and fields keep their usual gaps from pits and spoil heaps, as from each other. The clay deposits of the core land profile hold 1,800 kg a cubic metre, so one 3 m pit through a body 0.5 m to 2 m thick yields 2.4 to 29 t of clay, many households' pots for years.
- **Finding by cutting, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step, ADR-0010 §1):** a platform finds a buried body when its deepest cut, times the share done, reaches the body's cover and some of its plot lies over the body; its settlement records the builder as finder, and the chronicle says "Ash found clay while levelling a plot". The deepest cut need not lie over the body, a simplification. A pit is dug only on a body its settlement knows, so it finds nothing new.
- **Saves 21, wire 1.21 and techniques added since a save (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step):** saves schema 21 add to the `earth` section each earthwork's links to its deposit and its heap, and a person's target gains *deposit*. Saves of schema 20 load with every earthwork a platform, as they were. A technique founders bring that the content has added since a save was made is given on loading as a founder of each person's age would bring it, as a new skill is, and each settlement notes it as brought by its eldest knower; techniques the save named, known or lost, stay as they were. So a world saved before pottery loads with its potters. Wire 1.21 appends each earthwork's deposit and the kinds pit and spoil heap, which the observer draws dark and pale and describes ("Ada's household's clay pit, 1.2 m deep"; "the spoil heap of a clay pit, 3.1 m³"); someone digging is "digging clay at the clay pit north of home". The smoke notes count pits and what has been taken from deposits apart from levelled plots.
- **Pots only under a roof, and NUDGE: storage pots (2026-10-04, after slice Q's third step):** the step's first ten-year smoke was stopped at its fourth coast world. There, two of nine households went without a roof from year 2 to year 6, and the village dug 24 t of clay from one pit. Pots had been reckoned to keep anything, so a household with tonnes of grain in the open wanted a 15 kg pot for every 15 kg of it and spent its hours making them. A pot now keeps only what lies under a roof, turning room in lofts and on floors into raised room, and adds no room, so food in the open gains nothing from pots. A probe of the same world's first 30 months then found every household under a roof by its fourth month, but about a hundred 15 kg pots a household, made at 0.7 hours a day an adult in the first year. NUDGE: the core pot is now a large storage pot of 35 kg (about 45 litres, 8 kg of fired clay, 6 hours, 10 kg of clay and 12 kg of firewood, up to 5 a firing), within 07-05 §2.3's ranges. The research gives no size for a storage vessel; at this size the probe found about forty pots a household.
- **Slice Q's third step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, with pots under a roof):** all ten worlds pass. Five villages dug clay, in one or two pits each of 13 to 26 m³, and took 8 to 28 t of clay as it lay in the ground, before what was unfit for use went on the heaps (coast 3 and 4, river valley 1, 3 and 4). The other five found no clay they could reach and made no pots. Eight villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 20, 3: 34, 4: 13, 5: 31; river valley 2: 56, 3: 19, 4: 49, 5: 56), two more than the last smoke. Coast 1 dies out in year 8 and river valley 1 in year 6, and the ten worlds hold 278 people against the last smoke's 256. Pots change what households do with their hours, so these are other worlds than the last smoke's. One building gave way (coast 2's, killing 2). The economy checks: stocks green in nine and amber in one, asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six, amber in one and grey in three.
- **Daub pits, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's fourth step, ADR-0010 §2):** the earth a building's walls are daubed with is dug from a pit beside its plot, so no material comes without a visible source (11-12 §1.1: excavation never creates free material without a borrow pit, quarry face or ditch). The daub is the walls' infill, each group's area times its thickness, read from the grammar's groups, so the frozen hut grammar and its golden hash are untouched. It is dug in step with the walls stage's work as the walls go up, and in step with the share renewed when they are mended. Building and mending cost no more hours than before: the hut's 2 hours a square metre and the frame's 13.3 hours a cubic metre were always for digging, mixing and applying daub (11-04 §2.2's cob rate). The pit is begun when first needed, on the nearest free ground to the plot, behind it from the settlement's hearth before beside or in front of it. It is a square at least the people profile's pit side (3 m), sized so that its building's daub takes it about a metre down (a tuning value): a middling hut's 3 m pit goes down about 0.6 m. All its earth goes into the walls, so the ground loses it. A pit stays when its building is gone, and plots and fields keep their gaps from it. A building from a save before this step has no pit until its walls are next mended.
- **Quarries and flint pits, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's fourth step, ADR-0010 §2):** the land profile's deposit rules give `dig_h_per_m3`, the hours a capable adult takes to break a cubic metre of a body out of the ground, and `working`, what a working of it is called (content API 21). A body without a rate is dug as earth, at the people profile's 8 hours; its cover is always dug as earth. Stone is broken out at 12 hours a cubic metre with hammerstones, wedges and levers. At the rule's 0.1-0.6 fit for use, that is 20-120 hours a cubic metre of stone fit for use, against 11-08 §2.2's low-confidence envelope of 8-80 for small blocks from an easily worked, naturally jointed quarry. A flint bed is dug at 12 hours (tuning; no figure in the research). Two activities, quarrying stone and digging flint, dig them as clay is dug. They are weighed against gathering loose stone and flint, which brings up to 150 kg an hour where it lies thick, so people quarry only as the loose stone near a village is used up: it never renews. A test with no loose stone left sees a household that needs a quern quarry stone for it. The observer calls a stone working a quarry ("Ada's household's stone quarry, 0.8 m deep"), and someone at work there is "quarrying stone at the stone quarry west of home".
- **Slice Q's fourth step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, at the daub pits and quarries commit):** all ten worlds pass. Every village has its daub pits, 9 to 14 of them holding 38 to 72 m³ less the earth in its walls. Four villages dug clay, in one to three workings of 8 to 25 m³ (coast 4, river valley 1, 3 and 4). Four more began a working they left after 0.1 to 1.3 m³ (coast 1, 3 and 5, river valley 5): a household weighs a working by its body's average yield, cover and all, so it may begin one whose cover outlasts its need. Six villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 31, 3: 23, 5: 34; river valley 2: 55, 4: 49, 5: 52), against eight in the last smoke. Coast 1 dies out after year 7, coast 4 in year 10 and river valley 1 in year 6, and river valley 3 ends at 4. The ten worlds hold 248 people against the last smoke's 278. Daub pits and the gaps plots and fields keep from them move where households build and farm, so these are other worlds again. The last smokes swung as widely (coast 2 from 37 to 4 to 20 to 31), and nothing in this step costs anyone an hour, so nothing here points to it. Two buildings gave way, killing 1 each (coast 2 and river valley 5). The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality green in one and amber in nine, workshops green in six, amber in one and grey in three.
- **Cob walls deferred, and the oven next (2026-10-04, slice Q):** the plan's clay crafts for slice Q are pottery, an oven and cob walls. Cob is a mass-wall technique, not wattle and daub (11-08 §1.1: they must not share load paths merely because both contain earth). Its walls bear the roof, are raised in lifts that dry in between (11-04 §2.2: 1.5-4 person-days of eight hours a cubic metre, drying separate), and would need a mass-wall grammar with its own structural checks. That is a grammar beside the frame grammar, not a craft, so cob walls wait for a later milestone's mass-wall grammar. The clay oven follows as slice Q's next step.
- **The clay oven, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's fifth step):** baking in an oven is a technique founders bring (11-13 §1.2, §3.1: ovens in early farming houses at Çatalhöyük), learnt at home in 30 hours. An oven is a tool that stays where it is made (slice H's `fixed`, which was meant for it), so founders, and families the observer sends, bring none; a world loaded from a save before it gains the know-how but no oven. A household that knows how wants one for up to ten bakers. It builds it of 300 kg of clay and 30 kg of firewood with 25 hours of pottery's work (tuning values: the research gives no figure for an oven), so ovens are built only where a village knows clay it can dig. An oven lasts some 2,000 hours of baking (tuning). A firing heats it with three quarters of an hour of tending and 3 kg of wood, and up to 12 kg of flour bakes at 0.2 hours a kilogram, against the hearth's 0.39 hours and 0.5 kg of wood a kilogram. A full firing so takes half the hearth's wood a kilogram (07-06 §2.3: 5-15 % useful heat for an open fire, 15-30 % for an improved stove), and its 0.15 hours a kilogram of bread lie within 08-03 §2.2's 0.08-0.20 for a bakery's batch against 0.20-0.60 for a household's. A small batch costs more wood than the hearth would. People weigh the oven against the hearth as any two ways to the same food, and a household without an oven bakes at the hearth. A test sees founders without an oven dig clay, build one and bake in it, and the oven wear with use.
- **Slice Q's fifth step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, at the clay oven commit):** all ten worlds pass. Every village has its daub pits, 9 to 14 of them holding 44 to 72 m³. Five villages dug at deposits, in two to four pits and quarries of 17 to 35 m³ in all, and took 1.3 to 42 t as it lay in the ground (coast 1 and 4, river valley 1, 3 and 4). Three more began workings they left after 0.1 to 0.2 m³ (coast 3 and 5, river valley 5), as in the last smoke. Six villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 16, 3: 13, 5: 44; river valley 2: 58, 4: 44, 5: 53), as in the last smoke. Coast 1 dies out in year 8 and river valley 1 in year 6, coast 4 is down to 3 in year 10 and river valley 3 ends at 6. The ten worlds hold 237 people against the last smoke's 248. One building gave way, killing nobody (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in four, amber in one and grey in five. The smoke does not count ovens; the next one will.
- **Style v0, as built (2026-10-04, slice R's first step, ADR-0009 §7, research 11-02):** a style trait is a decision the grammars already expand: the roof's pitch, the height of a storey to the eaves and the roof's overhang. They change how a building looks, what it costs and what it carries, so taste meets structure with no special case: taller walls buckle sooner and a steeper roof carries more covering. The four parameters frame v1 keeps for style stay 0 until a grammar expands ornament, and a hut's overhang is its program's. A household's taste is the three, held to what each program allows whenever it builds (every core roof 45-55°: 11-01 §3.2, grass thatch sheds rain from 45°). Each founding band draws a way of building around the people profile's, keyed by its settlement, and each household its own around its band's; families the observer sends together draw one keyed by the first of them, so a group joining a village brings its own. A new couple's household takes the mean of the two it came from. Once a year, after wealth is recorded, each household meets each building its settlement finished in the year past that stands whole, other than its own, oldest first, and its taste moves toward it by `alpha` × admiration ÷ `prestige_most`. Admiration runs from 1 to `prestige_most` by the mean of two ranks among the settlement's: the owner's goods among its households and the building's mean part quality among the year's new buildings. 11-02 §1.1 warns that prestige is not wealth, and names patrons and builders both, so the craft counts as much as the patron. Each building is met once, so familiarity saturates (11-02 §2.2's first safeguard), and a building that gave way, or any part of it, is admired by nobody (§5.3: a collapse weakens a design's appeal). A household remembers the building that moved its taste most, and its next building records it as what it followed: provenance (§5.2). One commission in a hundred has one trait drawn anywhere its program allows, a keyed draw; that is taste innovation, not technique (§5.4). A household chooses its building by the usual design's cost and pays for the one built to its taste, as with caution. Values: `alpha` 0.1, `prestige_most` 3 and `innovation` 1 % lie inside 11-02 §2.2's test priors (0.02-0.20, 1-3 times, 0.1-3 %, all of low confidence). The tradition (48°, 1.9 m, 0.5 m), a band's spread (2.5°, 0.1 m, 0.1 m) and a household's (1°, 3 cm, 3 cm) are tuning values. Left for later: a repertoire of whole designs rather than three numbers (§5.1), exposure beyond a household's settlement through travel, trade and marriage (M5), and admiration by reputation rather than goods and craft (notables, M4).
- **Saves 22 and content API 22 (2026-10-04, slice R's first step):** saves schema 22 adds to each household its taste and the building it admired, and to each building the one its household's taste followed. Schema-21 and earlier saves load with each household's taste drawn as its band's would be, keyed by its settlement (one band to a settlement, so families sent earlier share their village's), nobody having admired anything and no building having followed one. A test loads a schema-21 save whose tastes are wrong and finds them drawn afresh. Content API 22 adds the people profile's `[style]`. The wire is unchanged in this step.
- **A partly built oven bakes nothing (2026-10-05, after slice Q's fifth step):** an oven is built over days, 25 hours of work in sessions of one to five hours, and a tool's stock counted any of it as a worn tool still at work (down to 2 % of one), so a household baked in an oven a twelfth built. A tool that stays where it is made now works only whole. One partly built does nothing, and one worn below whole is out of use until its household mends it with more clay, which it does while it keeps the usual quarter spare. Other tools still work to the last of a worn one. A test leaves the first household with an oven begun nothing to eat but flour, and finds it bakes at the hearth until its oven is whole; before the change it failed every time. The test had passed or failed by the order of simultaneous events, which differs from run to run (each world draws its own identity). It also found that a household of two bakes at the hearth even beside its oven, as a small batch costs less wood there (the oven step's intent), so the household it watches bake in the oven is made to know no other way.
- **Wire 1.22 (2026-10-05, slice R's second step):** `BuildingInfo` appends how the building was built, in the kernel's words ("roof pitched 49°, walls 1.9 m to the eaves, after Bo's hut"; a frame building's also "eaves 0.6 m out"), and the building its household's taste followed, which may since be gone ("after a building since gone"). `PersonInfo` appends, for the living, how their household would build and the building that moved its taste most ("roofs pitched 48°, walls 1.9 m to the eaves, eaves 0.5 m out; admiring Bo's hut"). The map's readout and the inspector show them.
- **Slice R's first step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-05, at the whole-oven commit):** all ten worlds pass. Each village builds its own way: roofs pitched 46-49° in one, 49-52° in another, two or three degrees apart within a village, with eaves of 1.8 to 2.1 m. Only 9 of the 109 buildings standing at the end were built after an admired one (coast 3: 4 of 9; river valley 3: 4 of 10; river valley 5: 1 of 13; none elsewhere). After the first year, most villages build almost nothing new: river valley 2 holds 58 people in 13 buildings. The few homes begun later are mostly young couples' first homes, and a new household admired nothing until its first review; the next entry changes that. Seven villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 15, 3: 22, 5: 25; river valley 2: 58, 3: 13, 4: 47, 5: 53), against six in the last smoke. Coast 1 ends at 4 (down to 6 in year 8) and coast 4 at 8, and river valley 1 dies out in year 6 again. The ten worlds hold 245 people against 237. Six villages dug at deposits, 14 to 34 m³ each, taking 9 to 42 t, and five hold ovens (1 to 13). Pots follow the food lying under roofs: coast 3's 22 people keep 508, river valley 3's 13 keep 237, and river valley 4's 47 keep 354. One building gave way, killing two (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six and grey in four.
- **A new household admires what its families did (2026-10-05, slice R):** a couple's new household took the mean of its families' tastes but admired nothing, so its first home, nearly all a village builds after its first year, followed nothing until the household's first review a year on. It now also takes the building the woman's household admired, else the man's, so a young couple builds after what their families admired (11-02 §5.2: people carry a few salient exemplars with them). A unit test covers the rule.
- **Caution words for huts (2026-10-05, found in the M3b demo runs):** a village whose hut lost its roof in a storm remembered the failure, and the knowledge panel said its roundhouses were "built 1.8 times as strong". Slice P sizes only frame buildings' members, so huts were built as they always had been. A hut's grammar has nothing to size. For a technique with no frame programs the panel now says "built as usual, as a hut has nothing to size: failures weigh 0.9 against 28 building-years".
- **The M3b demo (2026-10-05):** three river-valley worlds (seeds 2, 4 and 5, bands of 40 on 768-cell maps) lived 25 years each with `civ-host run --introduce core:technique/jointed_frame`: as each world began, the observer brought jointed timber framing to the eldest grown founder of its first household, as from elsewhere. The plan's three outcomes:
  - *A craft found by one person spreads or dies with its last practitioner.* In seed 4, Vela found drying and smoking in the tenth year, the only find in the 75 village-years. It was lost that same year with Kira, the last who knew it there, as 45 people left the village. Framing passed to children growing up in its knower's household (to four knowers in seed 2 by year 15, three in seed 4, two in seed 5), but nobody practised it, so it reached nobody beyond. It died with its last knower in all three: in seed 2 in year 19, when Isla, the last of four, died; in seed 4 with the households that left in year 10; in seed 5, where its first knower had died in year 11, when the whole village left in year 14.
  - *A loaded loft sags and fails, and builders over-build for a while.* No village built a frame building, so there was no loft to load (next entry). In seed 2 a hut lost its roof in a storm in year 3, its rafters poorly made. The village remembered it, and the memory faded over the years (failures weighing 0.9 against 28 building-years, then 0.1 against 78 by year 25). A hut has nothing to size, so nobody built stronger. After villages emptied, their abandoned huts lost their roofs and fell as they rotted. Loft failure and over-building are shown by slice P's test: a loft on badly made joists gives way under a full load, and the village builds its next longhouses' joists and posts stronger for years.
  - *New houses copy an admired one.* Every one of the 16 buildings begun after the first year was built after an admired building (6 in seed 2, 8 in seed 4, 2 in seed 5), each a young couple's first home following what their families admired. The two-to-five-degree spread of roof pitches within a village stayed as it was: a village builds little after its first year, and its founders' homes stand for decades.
  - The demo's pictures come from `web/e2e/m3b-demo.spec.ts` (`TCE_DEMO=1`), one ten-year world of seed 4 with its own identity. In the run pictured it holds 49 people, and framing is known by Gorran (45) and three young people of his household, practised by none. Five of its eleven buildings were built after an admired one, two each after Gorran's and Arrel's huts and one after Tam's, and the map's readout says so: "roof pitched 50°, walls 2.0 m to the eaves, after Gorran's hut". Two earlier runs of the same command had 50 and 53 people, with five and four such buildings.
- **NUDGE: no village builds a frame (2026-10-05, the M3b demo):** a household builds the cheapest home that covers its members and goods among those it knows how to build (slice O). In the demo's villages that was always a hut, and framing was never practised: the ten-year world's knowledge panel shows it known by four and practised by none, and nobody learnt it beside a knower in 75 village-years. So framing brought to a village spreads only to children at home, and no loft is loaded or fails. Whether pots, which keep grain under a roof as a raised floor does, took away the granary's use is not established. The structural rules are tested, but a frame building appears only where a household knows framing and needs more room than a hut gives. Not nudged here: the fix belongs with what makes frame buildings worth their cost (larger households, livestock and fodder, crafts that need room), not with a push toward them.
- **NUDGE: villages of about 55 leave together (2026-10-05, the M3b demo):** all three demo villages grew from 40 to 52-59 people and then emptied within a year: 45 of 57 people left seed 2 in year 17, 45 of 59 left seed 4 in year 10, and all 54 left seed 5 in year 14. This is the pattern of *founding risk over ten years* above (asking for food evens out stores, so households run out and leave together), now in grown villages. Its cause in these runs is not established. Revisit with M3c's soils and the dashboard's fifty-year runs. (Likely cause found the same day: *Villages fail in their seed's bad years*, below.)
- **Why river valley 1 dies out (2026-10-05, a time-boxed probe):** one seven-year run of the smoke's river valley seed 1 (1024-cell map) failed as *NUDGE: founding risk over ten years* describes. Its harvests rose to 9.5 t in year 3 and then fell to 8.6 and 7.3 t while its fields grew from 40 to 50 (10 to 12.5 ha): from about 950 kg a hectare to 590. Its grain, 5.1 t after the third harvest, was gone by the sixth spring, food ran short five times, and all 44 people left within the year. Labour was not short: adults sat at the hearth five to six hours a day, and fieldwork averaged under two. What lowered the yield per hectare (the years' weather, the ground of the newer fields, sowing dates) is not established; the run report gives no field-by-field yields. (The year's climate factor for seed 1 is 0.71 in year 5: see *Villages fail in their seed's bad years*, below.) The NUDGE's remedies stand, beginning with M3c's weather and soils, which will make the cause visible.
- **Villages fail in their seed's bad years (2026-10-05, found designing M3c):** the year's climate factor, which scales every harvest and the wild plants, is drawn from `[seed, climate, year]`, an AR(1) of correlation 0.3 and spread 0.25, with nothing of the landscape in the key. So both landscapes of a seed live through the same years, in every run. Recomputed with the kernel's own draws (a check outside the repository), the low years line up with every failure the log records. Seed 1 has 0.71 in year 5 and 0.42 in year 7: its river-valley band left in year 6, and both seed-1 bands have died out in every run since M1. Seed 2 has 0.59 in year 6, the M3a demo's sixth harvest "some 40 % short", and 0.65 and 0.75 in years 15 and 16, before its demo village emptied in year 17. Seed 4 has five lean years running (0.84, 0.80, 0.86, 0.71, 0.75 in years 6 to 10), and its village emptied in year 10. Seed 5 has 0.46 in year 13, and its village emptied in year 14. The NUDGEs above on founding risk, villages of about 55 leaving together and river valley 1 now have their likely cause: each seed's fixed lean years, met with thin reserves. Fragility is still the model's: households plan on an average year and keep little more. The fix to the keying belongs to M3c's weather, which draws each landscape's own days.
- **M3c design (2026-10-05):** five slices (§7), from four briefs drawn from twelve reports, with two ADRs: [ADR-0011](decisions/0011-execution-modes.md) on the speeds and their consistency, [ADR-0012](decisions/0012-weather-and-soil.md) on weather, field water and the soil. The order: speed first (S), because fifty-year runs of five worlds take hours at today's speed (a band of 40 lives a year in about 14 s); then the dashboard (T), so a baseline is logged before anything is tuned; then weather (U), which fixes the keying that gives every seed the same lean years; then soils (V), whose harvests need the water balance; then Accelerated mode in earnest, tuning and the demo (W). Each slice is usable and pushed on its own.
- **Plan §4.4's statistical day, amended (2026-10-05, ADR-0011):** §4.4 has Accelerated mode resolve each agent's day "statistically in one daily step". It now advances a day at a time through the same events, activities and decisions as Detailed mode (01-03 reads "one statistical step per day" as one request to advance to the next day). Its approximations are declared, can be switched off in tests, and must pass the statistical test: in M3c a household view built at midnight and leisure blocks. Samplers that replace per-event machinery (company from co-location pools, crime, fire) come later, one subsystem at a time. §4.4's mode-consistency test becomes two gates: exact equivalence on every push (Gate A), and statistical equivalence nightly (Gate B), its tolerances fixed from Detailed calibration runs before any result is seen. Why: a second, statistical rule set for every behaviour from M4 to M8 would invite the divergence §8 names as risk 6, and exact work plus two approximations is expected to reach the dashboard's budget; M8's 50k agents may need the samplers.
- **The dashboard's Gini band for hoe farmers (2026-10-05, slice T):** §4.7's band of 0.3–0.75 for the Gini of wealth fits farming states. M3c's villages hoe small fields with no plough, traction or livestock and no scarcity of land, and the research puts such communities lower: 08-14 §3.1 gives 0.27 ± 0.03 for horticultural populations against 0.48 ± 0.04 for agricultural ones (Borgerhoff Mulder and colleagues, weighted across embodied, relational and material wealth, not material wealth alone), and §3.2 asks that long-lived, relatively equal farming communities be possible. So until a world has the plough, traction, livestock or scarce land, the dashboard grades the Gini of goods, the median of years 26–50 in worlds lived in by ten or more: green within 0.3–0.75, amber from 0.1 to 0.3 with this reason, red below 0.1 (no difference at all, more likely a fault in sharing than a way of life) or above 0.75. The 0.1 is a tuning value; the research gives no floor.
- **Farming earthworks move to M6 (2026-10-05, ADR-0012 §7):** M3c's contents named irrigation ditches and farm terraces. They move to M6, beside its levees and floods. Terraces and drainage help only where their constraint binds (03-04 §2.8): their value comes from soil water and erosion, which M3c begins but does not finish, and the presets' rain already exceeds emmer's need, so a ditch would have nothing to do. A ditch is also a line with a gradient and upkeep (11-12 §5.1), unlike the rectangles of ADR-0010, whose "Revisit when" item moves with them.
- **Exact speed work (2026-10-05, slice S's first step, ADR-0011 §4):** a profile of a band of 40 lived on from a save put 92 % of the time in deciding, spread thin: looking over gathering places 17 %, the settlement's travel field 15 %, pricing a purchase 12 %, the tools a household wants 11 %. The household copied for each decision, which the design's probe had measured at 34 %, no longer showed. Four changes, each exact: the population's indexes and caches hash with the engine's fast hasher; the tools an activity needs are listed without allocating, and each technique is asked about once; the search over gathering places stops once even the best return expected or seen anywhere, so far off, could not beat the best found (places come nearest first, so the same place is found sooner); and the travel field skips cells already final and, like route searches, orders its open list by one packed integer in the same order as before. From one save each (seed 2 with 40 people, 90 days; seed 4 with 125 people, 60 days), every section of the save came out byte for byte as before, at 11.1 s a simulated year against 14.9 s, and 30.9 s against 37.6 s. With 125 people route searches now take a third of the time. The same check found that a world saved, loaded and lived on parts from one lived straight through (a save rounds the paths' wear and fades it to the day saved, and the routing view is drawn again on load): Gate A's save test needs that fixed, and it is slice S's next step.
- **Saves 23: worn ground kept exactly (2026-10-05, slice S, ADR-0011 §5):** a world saved at a midnight, loaded and lived on parted within a day from the same world lived straight through. A save kept each tile of worn ground faded to the day saved and rounded to 16 bits, and loading drew the routing view and the trails again from that, while the world never saved went on planning routes on the view of the month's survey. Saves schema 23 keeps each tile exactly as it stands, at its own day, and the routing view as surveyed (a byte a cell of the tiles it covers), and the trails are traced from the view, at a survey and on load alike. Nothing else needed keeping: every other cache is a pure function of saved state (a settlement's travel field depends only on the view and the ground, which no change of a run alters, and sites and plans are found afresh each day). Gate A's tests (`civ-sim/tests/modes.rs`) now show that a world saved at a midnight, loaded and lived 29 more days ends in the same bytes as one lived straight through, and that thirty days lived at once, a day at a time or cut every 433 minutes end the same. Both bench fixtures matched as well, saved after 33 days and lived 37 more. Saves are about the same size, as the worn ground is small beside the map. Saves of schema 22 and before load as they did, their routing view drawn again. The observer's paths show the routing view, as they always did, so a world loaded within a month of its making shows no trails until its first survey; before, loading drew them. Weather and soil now take saves 24 and wire 1.24 (ADR-0012 §6 renumbered).
- **Mending waits for spring's fieldwork (2026-10-05, a test that failed one run in 24):** the test of worn daub renewed gave its household 60 days to mend walls that had lost 60 % of their daub. Run over sixty worlds with fixed identities (a new hidden hook, `Sim::create_for_tests`), the walls were mended in 32 to 66 days, most near day 50 after a 1 March start: upkeep is wanted by the roof deadline in September, so sowing and preparing fields come first, as designed (ADR-0009 §4). The four slowest passed day 60. The test now allows 120 days; the behaviour is unchanged. Gate A's tests use a fixed identity, so they live the same world every run.
- **The day step and the Accelerated speeds, as built (2026-10-05, slice S's second step, ADR-0011 §1, §2, §6):** the speeds are 1×, 3× and 10× (Detailed) and 60×, 600× and Max (Accelerated), Max an infinite speed. The mode follows the speed and is a setting, not saved state. Every advance of the kernel now stops after the boundaries of the minute it stops at (at midnight, the land's and people's day) and before that minute's events, which come first in the next advance: the order is the one an uncut advance gives, so nothing a world lives changes, and a clock stopped at a midnight stands exactly where ADR-0011 puts a switch, after the day's work and before the new day's first event. A change of mode takes effect there: at once if the clock stands at a midnight, else at the next one. At 60× and 600× the host lives a whole day once a day's time has come, never more than one a tick; switched from a Detailed speed between midnights, it first lives the rest of the day at once. At Max it lives day after day for most of each 50 ms tick, at least one. So the clock stops, publishes and saves only at midnight. Accelerated frames show nobody on a trip, and the web and C++ observers stand the clock still between them rather than carry it and the walkers on. A saved speed is kept on load (the old clamp to 10× is lifted): Max as it is, others within 1× to 600×. Gate A's tests now also show that an Accelerated clock stops only at midnight and a change of mode is held for it, that three days at 10×, ten at Max and three at 10× end where sixteen at 10× do (no approximation is declared yet), and that a world saved at an Accelerated midnight lives on as if never saved. As yet Max is only as fast as the machine: a band of 40 lives about 11 s a simulated year here.
- **Wire 1.23 (2026-10-05, slice S):** the clock carries its mode (`ClockMode`: Detailed or Accelerated), and its speed may be infinite (Max), as may `SetClock`'s; the Welcome lists the Accelerated speeds as multiples of 1× after the Detailed ones (60, 600 and infinity). Old fields keep their meaning. The observer shows the six speeds, a rule between the two groups.
- **The sanity dashboard, as built (2026-10-05, slice T, plan §4.7):** `civ-host dashboard` lives five river-valley worlds (seeds 1 to 5, the content's regimes in turn, 1024 cells) fifty years each, four at a time on four cores. Each lives its first month by the minute with the smoke's checks of it, then a day at a time at Max. Every year's end makes the checks every long run makes, now shared with the smoke (`smoke::LongRun`: nobody stuck, no population or land problems, every good accounted for, households roofed from year 2, upbringing knowledge, a first trail, claims, fields and wealth measures in order); any that fails fails the dashboard. Then the §4.7 rows that apply at M3c are graded, thresholds only: **population** (3 of 5 worlds keep 10 people at the end, and no band above max(3, 1.05^y) times its founders by the end of year y; tuning, research 05-06 §5.2 and 05-01), **food prices** (in every world with two harvests or more to judge, grain asked more than 2 log points higher before the harvest than after, and stocks rising after one at least; tuning, beside 16-01 §2.2's 17-61), **wealth Gini** (the median of calendar years 26-50 of the largest settlement while it holds 10, judged over 5 such years at least: green 0.3-0.75, amber from 0.1 as hoe farmers may be, red beyond; entry above), **firm sizes** (the mean of workshops' lifetime hours above the median where a world has 10; 16-01 §2.1) and **structural failures** (failures of buildings lived in, per 1,000 years they stood lived in, at most 2 across all worlds; tuning, 11-06 §2.4's top scenario of 1 doubled; buildings nobody lived in are counted apart, as an emptied village's huts fall). Settlement sizes, crime, epidemics and regime labels are grey with their reasons and never count as passes. It prints the worlds and the rows, writes a JSON report with `--json`, keeps each world's saves at every tenth year with `--keep-saves`, and exits with failure on any red row or failed check. Five worlds and fifty years are fixed (`--years` gives a quicker look, said to be one); a failing run is not rerun until it passes (16-03 §4.7). A nightly job runs it and keeps the report; it is too long for pull requests until Accelerated mode is faster than Detailed. The statistical consistency test (Gate B, ADR-0011 §5) comes with the first approximation, in slice W: until then a day lived at Max is exactly a day lived by the minute (Gate A).
- **The dashboard's baseline (2026-10-05, slice T, before any tuning and before weather):** the first fifty-year run (the build of slice T, with the yearly climate factor) took 44 minutes on four cores, 199 to 2,640 s a world (the last measured while other work shared the machine), and FAILED on two rows. **Population, red:** two worlds of five keep ten people at the end, both under village fields: seed 2 (59, 46, 56, 52 and 48 people at years 10 to 50) and seed 4 (45, 49, 60, 72 and 78; at most 2.0 times its founders, in year 49, within the 1.05^y bound). The household-field worlds failed: seed 1 fell to 2 in year 6 (from 44 at most), seed 5 to 5 in year 13 (from 55) and seed 3 to 9 in year 13 (from 42), living on as 4 to 11 people. Each fell in its seed's lean years (*Villages fail in their seed's bad years*, above); that both survivors are village fields is confounded with their seeds. **Structural failures, red:** 4 lived-in failures in 1,846 lived building-years, 2.17 per 1,000 against at most 2 (two in seed 3's 122 years, two in seed 4's 804); buildings nobody lived in gave way 64 times. **Food prices, green:** grain asked 3-4 log points higher before each harvest than after, stocks rising after 130 of 131 harvests. **Gini, amber:** the median Gini of goods over years 26-50 was 0.27 in seed 2 and 0.33 in seed 4. **Firm sizes, green:** the mean of workshops' lifetime hours 1.2-1.9 times the median where a world had ten or more (seeds 2, 4 and 5). Settlement sizes, crime, epidemics and regimes grey. Nothing was tuned against it: weather (slice U) changes which years are lean, and soils (V) and the household view (W) come before any tuning.
- **Weather, as built (2026-10-05, slice U's first step, ADR-0012 §1, §2, §5):** `civ-land`'s `weather` module draws one daily series for each world from `[seed, weather, landscape, day]`, the landscape being its preset's id, the same at every speed. **Rain:** a two-state chain, its wet days by month (0.36-0.43 of days with 1 mm or more; research 03-03 §2.2 gives Cfb and Dfb 0.25-0.60 in the wet season, 0.08-0.45 in the dry) with persistence 0.3 (0.1-0.5); a wet day's amount 1 mm plus a gamma draw of shape 0.8 (0.5-2.0), each month's mean its share of the preset's annual precipitation (a summer maximum, as inland; tuning). **Temperature:** monthly normals at 150 m (January −1 °C, July 19 °C; tuning) plus an AR(1) anomaly of 0.7 (0.4-0.8) and 2.5-4 °C by month (Cfb 2-4, Dfb 3-6); a wet summer day 1 °C cooler than a dry one and every wet day's range narrower, each month's mean kept; 6.5 °C less for each km of height (§2.1). **The slow anomaly**, a monthly AR(1) of 12 months (§2.2: 6-36), scales wet-day amounts by exp(0.1s − 0.005), the share of wet days by 1 + 0.1s and May to September by −0.8 °C a unit, so wet and dry spells cross seasons with the mean kept (§1.6). **Snow** falls below a daily mean of 0.5 °C, lies in bands of 100 m and melts 3 mm of water a degree-day (tuning). **Soil water:** reference evapotranspiration from temperature (Hargreaves, FAO-56, at the people profile's latitude); the root zone holds 100 mm, drawn unstressed until 55 % is used (FAO-56's p; slice V's soils will set these by ground). The soil under the wild cover (crop coefficient 0.9) is the reference; each growing field keeps its own balance from the day it is sown to the day it ripens, starting from the reference's water that morning, with emmer's coefficients 0.4, 1.15 and 0.4 over stages of 30, 30, 40 and 20 days (03-02 §2.1 gives maize's 0.40 and 1.15; tuning). **The harvest** scales by 1 − 1.15 × (1 − water had ÷ needed) (08-02 §7.2, spring wheat), divided by that figure's long-run mean for the landscape, drawn over 300 years from the landscape's own stream when a world is made or loaded and never saved: 0.76 in the valley, so a season with all the water emmer needs gives 1.3 times the content's yield. **Wild plant food** grows by the cover's ease of drawing water, smoothed over a month, against what the month usually has; fallen wood, poles and reeds grow as authored. The yearly climate factor and its two parameters are retired. Saves schema 24 (an older save's weather starts from the old year's deviate as its slow anomaly, with the soil at field capacity, no snow, and a crop growing then unstressed so far); content API 23 (`[weather]`, `follows_water`, a crop's `kc`, `kc_days` and `ky`). **Measured:** 500 years of the valley give 803 mm a year (CV 0.22) on 139 wet days, a mean of 9.2 °C, 101 frost days and 37 days with snow lying on the valley floor, and a harvest factor of mean 1.01 and CV 0.22 (08-02 §7.3: 0.20-0.35 for rainfed farming in a favourable climate, all causes together), below 0.8 in 21 % of years and below 0.6 in 3.6 % (the retired factor: 21 % and 5.5 %). The coast's 1,019 mm give a harvest CV of 0.18. A year's rain correlates with the year before's at 0.32 (0.37 on the coast), more than most stations show (the slow anomaly; tuning), and a harvest with the one before at 0.12 (the retired factor: 0.3). The first fifty years of seeds 1 to 5 average 747-879 mm and harvest factors of 0.93-1.06; seed 1's first twelve years are dry (728 mm, a factor of 0.89). `civ-host weather --seed N` replays a world's weather on its valley floor without making the world, and `civ-host run` reports each year's with the water its crops had. Still to come in slice U: the observer's weather (wire 1.24), workable days, wear by wetness, storms and snow on roofs, and prices that answer stores.
- **NUDGE: late sowing is counted twice in part (2026-10-05, slice U):** emmer still loses its authored 0.5 % a day of sowing after 21 March, a tuning value that stood for everything a later season costs. A later crop now also meets the drier weeks of July and August in its water balance. How much of the authored loss the water now carries is not measured; revisit in slice W's tuning, not before. *Measured the same day:* the water balance alone gives emmer sown on 5 May 6.2 % less than emmer sown on 21 March in the valley, and 3.3 % less on the coast, against the authored 22.5 %: about a quarter and a seventh of the authored loss is counted twice.
- **Wire 1.24 (2026-10-05, slice U):** the clock carries today's weather on the valley floor (`DayWeather`: rain and snow, mean, lowest and highest temperature, snow lying, the soil water under the wild cover, and the kernel's words: "9 °C, rain on dry ground"); the snapshot a `weather_rev` that changes each day lived; `GetWeather` answers a `WeatherReport` of every month lived (its rain and the month's long-run mean, wet days, mean temperature and its normal, lowest and highest, frost days, days with snow lying, and mean soil water) with today's weather; and a field says the share of its water need its growing crop has had (`water_had`, −1 before it has needed any) and its root zone's water (`soil_water`). Old fields keep their meanings. The observer shows the words on the clock and a weather panel of the last twelve months and each calendar year against the usual; the field readout's status says how much of the water it needed a crop has had.
- **Slice U's first step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-05, at the weather commit):** nine of ten worlds pass, in 14.5 minutes. Coast 4 fails the roof check: two of its nine households lived without a roof from year 2 to the end, with 47 people at year 10 (next entry). Coast 5 dies out (down to 3 in year 3) and river valley 1 ends with 4 (down to 3 in year 5); the others keep 11 to 56 people (coast 1: 20, 2: 26, 3: 11; river valley 2: 56, 3: 11, 4: 45, 5: 43), 263 in the ten worlds against 245 in slice R's smoke, under other weather. One building gave way, killing nobody (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks green in all ten; asks green in nine and grey in one; inequality green in one, amber in eight and grey in one; workshops green in six and grey in four.
- **A worn-out axe could not be replaced (2026-10-05, probing coast 4):** a probe of coast 4's first year under a fixed identity found the two roofless households' huts at their first stage from days 92 and 99, waiting for poles. Each household held 2 % of an axe, no timber and 0.4 kg of flint; its adults left cutting poles out for want of a tool, and building for want of materials. An axe needs a kilogram of timber for its haft, and timber came only from cutting poles, which needs an axe: a household whose axe wore out with no timber in store could never make another, and nothing in the village broke the loop. The core pack adds `core:activity/cut_rods`: thin rods and saplings cut with a flint flake or broken by hand, at a fifth of an axe's rate (research 07-05 §1.3: a usable cutting flake needs little finishing; 03-06 §2.5: thin rods as well as poles; the rate is a tuning value, as the research gives none). The kernel's rule that leaves slower field work out while a tool for faster work is free now covers gathering, more strictly: a slower way to gather a resource is left out while the household holds a tool for a faster one, even in another member's hands. Cutting by hand whenever the household's axe was busy took hours from other work (two fewer fields in one probe's first season) and held back a household's first hut in a test, so it is kept to households without an axe; reaping by hand beside a busy sickle stays, as a ripe crop cannot wait. Under the same two identities coast 4 then roofs all nine households in its first year: one lives exactly as before, as none of its households ever lacked an axe, and the other harvests 2 to 4 % more over three years (4.2, 7.4 and 12.5 t against 4.1, 7.3 and 12.3) with the same people.
- **Workable days and the farmers' view (2026-10-05, slice U's second step, ADR-0012 §4, §5):** work that turns the soil, breaking and preparing ground and sowing, waits for a day the ground can be worked (as first built, all field work out of doors waited: below). Snow lying at a field's height (5 mm of water or more), 5 mm or more of rain or snow in the day (the weather profile's `wet_ground_mm`, a tuning value) or a daily mean below freezing at its height (`frozen_below_c`) keeps people from it, and their choices say why ("the ground too wet to work", "snow on the ground", "the ground frozen"); weeding, reaping and threshing go on whatever the weather. From its 300 years the climatology measures, for each crop, the share of the days of its window for preparing and sowing that can usually be worked on the valley floor: 81 % in the valley and 76 % on the coast. Households plan their spring work on those days (next entry), and the urgency of preparing and sowing counts only the days left that can be worked, so the work presses harder. A growing crop is judged by the water its season so far has brought (§4: people see the season so far, never future draws): its expected harvest, by which tending and reaping are weighed and which the field readout shows, is an average year's for the rest of the season and what its water so far allows for the share of a season's usual need already passed (417 mm in the valley, 414 on the coast). `civ-host weather` prints each year's workable days of the window.
