Content API 24 (`wet_ground_mm`, `frozen_below_c`, `peak_work_hours_per_day`). **Measured:** a probe of coast 4's first two years by the hour, under a fixed identity, found a fifth less preparing in March and a fifth more in April, and the season's hours of preparing and sowing within 1 % of before; the first harvest was 4 % smaller with the same 25 fields, and the second 10 % smaller (43 fields against 45), sown later and reaped over more wet days. Over four years under two identities coast 4 harvested 3.9-4.0, 6.5-6.6, 10.1-10.3 and 11.0-11.3 t (before: 4.1-4.2, 7.3-7.4, 12.3-12.5 and 12.1-12.7) and kept 37 to 45 people (41 to 44). River valley 1 harvested 3.4-3.6, 7.4-7.9 and 6.8-6.9 t (3.7-3.8, 8.2-8.5 and 7.3), 3 to 13 % less, from fewer hectares under one identity and smaller yields under the other, and in its fourth year fell to 6 and 11 people where before it kept 44 and 42: it was already the most fragile world, down to 3 in year 5 in the last smoke.
- **NUDGE: spring plans count on peak hours (2026-10-05, slice U):** planning spring work on the days that can be worked at ordinary hours (6 a day) shrank the area a household would take on by the days lost, about a fifth. In that first build coast 4's first two harvests fell by 14 to 23 %, and under both identities probed its households left the valley in year 3, 6 people of 44 staying, where before it kept 41 to 44. Research 04-02 §2.4 gives 10 hours of field work at a peak against 6 ordinarily, so a household now plans on the days that can usually be worked at peak hours, never on more than every day's ordinary work: while three days in five or more can be worked, the plan is what it was, and the weather takes only the days themselves. The people profile's `peak_work_hours_per_day` carries the figure. Whether people really give the longer hours on the days they can work is not measured: the probe found the season's hours of preparing and sowing unchanged, only later.
- **Workable days narrowed to the work that turns the soil (2026-10-05, slice U):** the ten-year smoke at the workable-days commit FAILED. River valley 4 sowed no field in its first 30 days (11 marked out): snow lay on its valley floor on 65 days of its first year, and only 42 of that year's 67 days of the window for preparing and sowing could be worked. Only 3 of the 9 bands that lived on kept ten people (coast 2: 28, coast 4: 45, river valley 2: 60), against 8 of 10 in the weather commit's smoke: coast 3, coast 5 and river valley 3 died out, and coast 1, river valley 1 and river valley 5 ended with 6 to 8 people. CI's month-long smoke failed on river valley 4 too. A probe of the four worlds that fell (coast 1 and 3, river valley 3 and 5, one identity, six years) against the build before workable days found holding back weeding and reaping on wet days did most of the harm (coast 1: 6 people in year 6, against 21; river valley 5: 3, against 41). Judging crops by their water so far made no consistent difference. Weeding goes on in wet weather in practice, and the model has no wet grain or drying to make a wet day at harvest cost more than the time lost, so only breaking and preparing ground and sowing now wait for the ground. Even so, workable days cost the most fragile worlds: in the probe coast 3 left in year 4 (34 people at year 6 before) and river valley 5 in year 5 (41 before), coast 1 kept 20 (21), and river valley 3 fell in year 6 either way. Without workable days these worlds are already marginal: coast 1 halved in year 5 and river valley 3 fell in year 6. The smoke's first-month check now asks for a sown field only when the ground could be worked on at least 15 of its first 30 days, 3 of them in the sowing window (`smoke::WORKABLE_DAYS`): river valley 4 could work its ground on 11, the other worlds on 18 to 29. Survival is the dashboard's to answer: households plan their fields on the content's yield, not on what their fields have given, which slice V's field records change, and slice W tunes.
- **Slice U's second step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-05, with workable days narrowed to the work that turns the soil):** all ten worlds pass, and at the edge: 5 of 10 bands keep ten people (coast 1: 12, coast 2: 32, coast 4: 47; river valley 2: 59, river valley 4: 49), the fewest the check allows. Coast 3, coast 5, river valley 3 and river valley 5 die out (in years 4, 3, 6 and 5) and river valley 1 ends with 3: 202 people in the ten worlds against 263 before workable days. River valley 4's first month, with 11 days the ground could be worked, no longer fails it. One building gave way, killing two (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks green in nine and grey in one; asks green in seven and grey in three; inequality green in one, amber in six and grey in three; workshops green in two and grey in eight. 11.7 minutes, while other work shared the machine.
- **Wear by wetness (2026-10-05, slice U, ADR-0012 §5):** on the first of each month thatch, daub and the feet of posts wear as wet as the month just lived was against its usual on the valley floor: a month with twice its usual rain and snow wears them twice as fast, and one with half of it half as fast, from a quarter (a month without rain still wears, in sun and wind) to three times (tuning values). Rafters and joists still rot only as the roof over them leaks. Over 300 years of the valley a month brings 1.01 of its usual on average, more than twice it in 3.0 % of months and less than a quarter in 1.5 % (the coast: 0.99, 2.8 % and 2.4 %), so the authored rates hold on average, and a year's wear runs from 0.59 to 1.65 of an average year's (standard deviation 0.20; the coast 0.51 to 1.71). No content or saves change.
- **Wire 1.25 (2026-10-07, slice U):** today's weather (`DayWeather`) appends the snow line, the lowest height at which snow lies: the foot of the lowest 100 m band with 5 mm of water or more lying, and infinite when it lies nowhere (as an older host's weather reads). The observer shades the map's land for the season the clock tells. It dulls toward grey-brown from September to April, most in December and January; from April to October it yellows as the soil water under the wild cover on the valley floor falls below 60 % of what the soil holds; and above the snow line it is white. The tints are the observer's, for showing only: the kernel gives the month, the soil water and the snow line. The map shades its terrain again only when the month, the soil water by tenths or the snow line by 50 m changes, at most every two seconds. Water, fields, worn ground and buildings are drawn as before.
- **The weather in the chronicle, saves 25 (2026-10-07, slice U, ADR-0012):** on the first of each month the chronicle notes the month just lived if its weather on the valley floor stood out against what it usually brings: rain and snow at twice the month's long-run mean or more (wet) or at a quarter of it or less (dry), or a mean temperature 3 °C or more below its normal (cold) or above it (warm), in the kernel's words ("October was wet and cold on the valley floor: 168 mm fell, 2.1 times what October usually brings; a mean of 3.5 °C, 6.0 °C below its usual."). On the first of May it notes the winter from October if snow lay on the valley floor on 1.75 times the days a winter usually has or more (long), or on a quarter of them or fewer (mild), and at least 10 days from the usual either way, so a landscape where snow seldom lies has neither mild winters nor long ones of a few days. On the first of January it notes the calendar year if its rain and snow came to 1.3 times its usual or more (wet) or to 0.7 of it or less (dry). The climatology also measures the usual days of snow lying by month (derived, never saved). The thresholds are tuning values, chosen so that what is noted stands out: over 500 years of the valley, 543 months were noted (9 %: 193 wet, 107 dry, 102 cold, 154 warm), 69 winters (14 %: 48 long, 21 mild) and 83 years (17 %: 51 wet, 32 dry), about 1.4 entries a year. Snow by the month was measured and left out: ten days more of snow lying than usual marked a fifth of Januaries, as in deep winter snow lies all month or hardly at all, so a winter is judged whole. The entries say what the weather was, never what it did (research 15-02 §1.2 keeps observation apart from explanation), and they come whoever lives in the world. Saves schema 25 adds the chronicle's code 24; saves of schema 24 load as they are.
- **Storms and snow on roofs (2026-10-07, slice U, ADR-0012 §5):** roofs carry the weather, and the monthly draw for each settlement that stood in for it is retired. **Storms:** once a month a storm crosses the world's landscape, one draw from the weather's own key (`[seed, weather, landscape]`) for each month, on one day of it (1 to 28), the same for every roof under it. Its gusts load a roof log-normally about 0.25 kPa on its plan with a spread of 0.6, the stand-in's figures, now wind alone: one month in twenty above about 0.7 kPa, one in a thousand above about 1.6 (research 11-05 §2.4: q = ρv²/2, about 0.25 kPa at 20 m/s and 1 kPa at 40 m/s; tuning values). They are the same in every month, as the research gives no season of storms. **Snow:** a roof carries the snow lying on the ground at its building's height, its water's weight (11-05 §2.4: p = ρgd from the snow lying), of which a roof keeps 0.8 when pitched at 30° or less, falling to none at 60° (tuning values: the research gives no share): a hut's 45-55° thatch keeps 0.13 to 0.4 of it. Over 500 years the year's deepest snow on the valley floor holds a median 33 mm of water, 68 mm one year in ten and 108 mm one in a hundred (1.06 kPa on the ground); at 350 m, 46, 91 and 142 mm. So a hut's roof carries about 0.1 kPa of snow in an ordinary winter and up to about 0.4 in a hard one, beside the month's storm on its day. A building that gives way under more snow than storm, with 0.1 kPa or more of snow on its roof, is told of as giving way "under snow". Content API 25 moves the figures into the weather profile (`storm_median_kpa`, `storm_spread`, `roof_snow_share`, `roof_snow_full_deg`, `roof_snow_shed_deg`) and retires `[peak_load]`; no saves or wire change (the storm is a keyed draw; the snow lying is saved already). **Ten-year smoke** (9.9 minutes): all ten worlds pass, at the edge as before: 5 of 10 bands keep ten people (coast 1: 42, coast 2: 25, coast 4: 48; river valley 2: 59, river valley 4: 46), coast 3, coast 5 and river valley 5 die out and river valleys 1 and 3 end with 3 and 4: 227 people in the ten worlds against 202 in the last smoke, under other draws of who the founders are. Two buildings gave way, killing nobody (coast 2, river valley 2), against one in the last smoke: no sign that the failure band moved, which the next dashboard run measures. The economy checks: stocks green in all ten; asks green in seven and grey in three; inequality green in one, amber in six and grey in three; workshops green in three and grey in seven.
- **Grain asks that answer stores (2026-10-07, slice U's last step, ADR-0006 §4's stock term):** a household's ask for food answers what it holds beyond its needs. Its anchor, its own cost and margin, is multiplied by exp(−0.5 × s), `s` the years of its own need it can spare, at most one, so a household with a year's food to spare asks 0.61 of its anchor (research 08-04 §1.2: a cost anchor with modest inventory feedback; the 0.5 is a tuning value, as the research gives none). The spare is measured in years of need rather than against the store kept to the next harvest, so a store drawn down as planned does not move the ask (08-05 §1.5 warns of that artifact); the ask still moves at most 5 % a review. Content API 26 (`market.stock_response`). **Probe:** river valley 2 under village fields, two fixed identities, eight years, with the term and without. Without it the median grain ask stood at 1.845 hours a kg after every harvest and 1.90 before, whatever the harvest (99 to 339 kg a head). With it, asks after the harvest fell as good harvests (308 to 339 kg a head) filled the stores, from 1.76 and 1.79 in year 1 to 1.12 by year 7, and under one identity rose again after the leaner harvests of years 4 (174 kg a head: 1.46 to 1.53) and 8 (249: 1.12 to 1.21); under the other they held after years 4 and 8 (186 and 233 kg a head), its sellers still holding a year or more to spare. Harvests and head counts were the same under both, year by year: in eight years the asks changed nothing about who ate. **Ten-year smoke** (10.5 minutes): all ten worlds pass. Grain is asked 6 to 16 log points more before the harvest than after in the seven worlds with enough harvests to judge, and 47 in one over two harvests, against 3 to 4 before (research 16-01 §2.2: 17-61 in markets that also carry credit and risk). Populations as before, at the edge: 5 of 10 bands keep ten people (coast 1: 21, coast 2: 29, coast 4: 48; river valley 2: 61, river valley 4: 47), coast 3, coast 5 and river valley 1 die out, and river valleys 3 and 5 end with 4 and 5: 215 people in the ten worlds. Two buildings gave way, one killing one person (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks green in nine and grey in one; asks green in eight and grey in two; inequality green in two, amber in six and grey in two; workshops green in five and grey in five. With this step slice U is complete.
- **The soil, as built (2026-10-07, slice V's first step, ADR-0012 §3):** each field keeps its soil's nitrogen in two pools, kilograms a hectare: humus turning over at 0.02 a year and roots, stubble and fresh organic matter at 0.3, RothC's base rates for humified and resistant plant matter (research 03-04 §2.5). Native ground of average richness holds 2,600 kg in humus (03-04 §2.1 works 0.2 m of soil at 1.3 t/m³, 1 % organic carbon and a C:N of 10 to 2.6 t) and 100 kg in the fast pool (tuning), both scaled by the field's ground. On the first of each January, with no draws, each pool releases its share, which with 15 kg from the air and free-living microbes (tuning: the research gives no pre-industrial figure) is the year's supply, and last year's unused supply is lost: native ground releases 82 kg a year (03-04 §2.1: 39-91). A crop takes up 0.6 of the supply (03-04 §4.2: 51-68 % of fertilizer nitrogen recovered with enough P and K) and emmer 0.035 kg for each kilogram of grain it grows (tuning: grain 0.020 and straw 0.006 a kilogram, 03-04 §2.2, with 1.5-2 kg of straw a kilogram of grain and the roots), so native ground allows about 1,660 kg/ha, above what any season gives. A harvest is the least of what its season and its soil allow (03-04 §5.2). The grain and the straw carried home take their nitrogen off the field, and the rest of what the crop took up, its stubble and roots, returns to the fast pool. A field that gave no harvest the year before grew its wild cover, which brings each pool back toward native ground's at the pool's own rate: nitrogen is never made, and cropping every year runs a field down over decades toward what the air's nitrogen keeps, about 260 kg/ha, never to nothing (03-04 §4.1; in a scratch model of one field, harvests in the fifth decade were about 80 % of the first decade's and in the tenth 53 %, while two crops and a rest year held about 90 %). Each field remembers its last eight harvests per hectare and which of the two held each back. Saves schema 26 (an older save's fields are cropped from native ground of their richness for as many years as each has given harvests, with none on record); content API 27 (`[soil]`, a crop's `grain_n_kg_per_kg`, `straw_n_kg_per_kg` and `crop_n_kg_per_kg`). `civ-host run` reports each year's harvests per hectare, how many the soil held back and the soil's supply against native ground's. **Ten-year smoke** (8.4 minutes): FAILED by one band. Four of the nine bands that lived on kept ten people (coast 2: 20, coast 4: 47; river valley 2: 60, river valley 4: 49), coast 5 died out and coast 1, coast 3 and river valleys 3 and 5 ended with 7, 4, 4 and 3, and river valley 1 failed its first month: on day 30, 5 of its 8 households had no firewood. Neither is the soil's. Nothing in the first month touches it (no harvest, no turn), and river valley 1's first month passed in eight more runs of this build under fresh identities. Ten years of coast 1 and river valley 5 (768-cell maps) gave 0 harvests held back by the soil in the 4 and 6 years they cropped, the soil's supply falling to 87 % and 78 % of native ground's; both bands left (coast 1 in years 3 to 5 with harvests of 637 to 981 kg/ha, river valley 5's 47 people at once in year 7 after a 600 kg/ha year). Coast 1 has ended between 6 and 42 people in the smokes of slice U. The economy checks: stocks green in eight and grey in one, asks green in eight and grey in one, inequality amber in eight and grey in one, workshops green in four and grey in five. Survival stays at the edge, as the dashboard has measured since its baseline; slice V's next step lets households plan from what their fields have given.
- **Planning from the fields (2026-10-07, slice V's second step, ADR-0012 §4):** households plan from what they can see, never the soil. A field is expected to give the mean of its last three harvests a hectare within five years, or with none, what the crop gives on ground of its quality (tuning values). A household's need is grain, not area: the grain its members eat in a year with what is lost before it is eaten. It crops the fields already begun, then the others best first, by the grain each is expected to give beyond its seed (at the cautious share it plans on) for the work it needs before it is sown, until they are expected to grow that need; the rest rest (research 08-02 §6: the land a household holds is not the land it sows). It marks out new ground only when all its fields cropped would fall short. New ground, and ground grown over, can be broken on any day the ground can be worked, out of the season too: broken out of it, it lies broken until the window, when it is dug like cropped ground. A household breaks ground out of season when it has the seed for everything it means to crop, can break the new ground before the window opens and can dig and sow it all in the window; nothing presses on that work until the window. Broken ground left unsown three whole years has grown over and is broken again (not cleared again) before it is cropped (content API 28, `[soil] regrown_years`; a tuning value). The communal allocation of village fields and the seed set aside plan at the yield each household's fields have given. The observer calls such ground "grown over, to be broken again", and `civ-host run` counts it. **Ten-year smoke** (9.6 minutes): all ten worlds pass, and all ten bands keep ten people (in the later smokes of slice U, five did): coast 1: 51, coast 2: 48, coast 3: 49, coast 4: 48, coast 5: 17; river valley 1: 52, 2: 55, 3: 48, 4: 46, 5: 54, 468 people in the ten worlds against 215 to 227 in the smokes of slice U. Breaking ground in the autumn takes its 1,000 hours a hectare out of the spring, which had bound the area sown since the workable days; households now hold and crop much more ground. In ten years of river valley 5 (768-cell map) its households held 68 fields at the end of year 1, 27 of them reaped that year, and 115 in year 10, 99 reaped (about 0.48 ha a person, 751 kg/ha): grain 20 to 35 % above their need in an average year, the buffer that planning on a cautious share of what fields gave implies (10-01 §2.3). The soil now begins to tell: its supply fell to 77 % of native ground's by year 10, and in year 9 it held back 4 harvests. Coast 1's households, 37 people after 16 left in year 2, held 69 fields by year 7, reaping 57 to 62 of them, and let 1 to 3 grow over. Three buildings gave way, each killing one person (coast 1, 2 and 3), against one or two in each smoke of slice U, as larger villages store more: the dashboard measures their rate. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in eight and amber in two. Survival is no longer at the edge in ten years; the fifty-year dashboard has not yet been run with soils, and slice W's tuning answers to it.
- **Middens and manuring (2026-10-07, slice V's last step):** each household keeps a midden beside its home, settled on the first of each month and whenever it is drawn on: each member adds 0.5 kg a day of ash, food waste, sweepings and dung holding 1 kg of nitrogen a year, and the heap loses half of itself and its nitrogen to the air and the rain in a year (tuning values; research 12-02 §2.3: a person's faeces hold 0.42 kg a year and their urine 3.56, most of which soaks away; 12-02 §3: early farming middens mixed domestic waste, ash and fecal material). A test holds the content to a share below the 4 to 5 kg of nitrogen in a year of the grain a person eats, so a midden returns part of what harvests took and never makes nitrogen (03-04 §5.5). Founders bring `core:technique/manuring` (07-04 §4.4: documented in Neolithic Europe by the sixth millennium BCE), which gates `core:activity/manure_field` and the new field task `manure`. A household carries dung, in 25 kg loads there and back with 2 hours a tonne to dig out and spread it (research 08-12: a porter carries 15-30 kg; tuning), to a field it means to crop that lies broken or dug and has given less than new ground of its kind, the one where an hour brings most first, so near fields are dunged before far ones (03-04 §2.3: intensive manuring is concentrated near settlements). People value it by what dung is known to bring the next harvest, never by the soil: its nitrogen, the tenth the year's crop draws on, taken up at 0.6 and over the crop's 0.035 kg a kilogram of grain, up to the field's shortfall from new ground's. The dung enters the pools, 0.3 of it the fast one (content API 29: `[soil] manure_fast_share`), so a tenth of its nitrogen reaches the year's crop wherever in the year it is spread (03-04 §2.3: 5-15 %) and the rest feeds later years. In the first build households took up dung as soon as the heap held a load, while a session runs an hour at least: about 0.1 hours an adult a day went to carrying, ten times what the heaps held. Manuring now waits until the heap holds an hour's carrying. Saves schema 27 (a household's midden; older saves begin it empty on loading); content API 29 (`[midden]`, `manure_fast_share`, the technique and the activity). The observer tells each field's last harvest and whether its soil held it back ("fallow; last gave 512 kg/ha in year 7, held back by its soil"), and `civ-host run` what the middens hold. **Ten-year smoke** (17.8 minutes, beside the dashboard): all ten worlds pass and all ten bands keep ten people (coast 1: 54, 2: 27, 3: 54, 4: 50, 5: 17; river valley 1: 54, 2: 58, 3: 48, 4: 41, 5: 57; 460 people); one building gave way, killing nobody; stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in nine and grey in one. In ten years of river valley 5 (768 cells) its middens held 0.3 to 0.8 t at each year's end against the 11 to 12 t they would hold if none were carried out: households carry out nearly all their dung, about 8 t a year, which at about 2 kg of nitrogen a hectare a year over the 25 ha they crop leaves the soil's drawdown as it was (its supply at 77 % of native ground's by year 10). At hand scale and village size a midden matters on the fields it is carried to, not across the village's ground, as research 03-04 §2.3 leads one to expect. With this step slice V is complete.
- **The dashboard with soils (2026-10-07, after slice V's second step, `269edf3`):** the fifty-year dashboard lived its five river-valley worlds (1024 cells, the regimes in turn) in 75.8 minutes on four cores, beside other work, and FAILED one row. **Population green**, for the first time: all five worlds keep ten or more after fifty years, with 82 to 87 people from 40 founders (50 to 59 at year 10, 75 to 88 at year 40), the most growth 2.3 times the founders (world 2, year 44), within the bound; the baseline (§9, slice T) had two worlds of five keep ten, and `main`'s nightly dashboards of 2026-10-05 to -07, before soils, kept 0, 2 and 1. Food prices green in every world (grain asked 3 to 7 log points more before the harvest than after, stocks rising after every harvest). The Gini of goods amber: 0.27 to 0.40, two worlds within the band of 0.3 to 0.75 and three just below it, as hoe farmers with land to spare may be (research 08-14 §3.1: horticultural populations 0.27 ± 0.03). Firm sizes green (155 to 273 workshops a world). **Structural failures RED:** 11 failures of lived-in buildings in 4,635 building-years, 2.37 per 1,000, against at most 2 (the baseline: 2.17), and none of empty buildings besides. A world now takes 33 to 76 minutes, the village worlds the longest, as villages hold more people and fields than the baseline's. Slice W's time-boxed tuning answers to the failures; the middens' step came after this run.
- **Accelerated mode's approximations, as built (2026-10-07, slice W, ADR-0011 §4 amended):** measured on a river-valley world lived twenty years (seed 1, 1024 cells; 65 people, 121 fields, 16 roofed huts), 90 to 120 days from its save with `bench_village`. A profile of 30 days at Max with leisure blocks put 24 % of the time in rebuilding a settlement's walking times (a Dijkstra over the whole map, as hunting walks two hours), 12 % in planning routes, 8.5 % in each day's expected yields, 8 % in finding the best purchase and 5 % in finding the best place to gather. About 14.5 decisions are made a person-day. **Exact:** a settlement's walking times are rebuilt when the paths are surveyed or the hearth moves; the monthly refresh on the same survey now finds only the ground that could be broken again. Detailed went from 60.7 to 56.8 ms a day, every section's digest unchanged in both modes. **The household view** keeps what a household's members weigh whoever decides: the purchase, whom to ask, paid work, the best place for each gathering, digging and field activity. It is kept from the household's first decision after midnight until midnight or its own consequential step: a deposit, work on a field or a building, making, trying, or a choice that lays claim to something. Every household's view is refreshed after a gift, a trade or paid work. Alone it made a day 20 % faster (45.6 against 57.2 ms a day over 120 days), with time use unchanged within 0.01 h a person-day. **Leisure blocks** alone made a day 15 % faster (48.7 ms a day); both together 23 % (44.2 ms a day), about 16 s a simulated year. `max_minutes` of rest, play and the hearth (180, tuning values; Detailed mode never uses them) caps a block. Both approximations are set aside at midnight, so Gate A's save at an Accelerated midnight still lives on exactly; with both switched off (`Sim::set_approximations`), ten days at Max still end in the bytes of ten by the minute.
- **Gate B's tolerances (2026-10-07, set before Accelerated mode was evaluated, ADR-0011 §5):** `civ-host consistency --calibrate` lived each fixture (river valley seed 1 and ria coast seed 2, 768 cells, fixed identities, lived by the minute to 1 January of year 3) eight times in Detailed mode for a year, each with its own tie-break stream, in 1.8 minutes on four threads; every run passed the long runs' checks. The river valley barely varies when only the order of an instant's events is redrawn: 44 people in every run, work 2.625 ± 0.005 h a person-day. The coast varies more: 41.6 ± 0.5 people, work 3.00 ± 0.15 h, materials ± 7 %. Each aggregate's tolerance bounds the difference of the two modes' means as a share of the Detailed mean. It is the larger fixture's three standard errors of a difference of means of five runs each, at the Detailed spread, rounded up to a whole percent and at least 3 %. The roofed share's floor is 10 %, one household in about ten. **The tolerances:** people 3 %, work 10 %, leisure 3 %, sleep 3 %, walking 9 %, food 4 %, harvest 4 %, materials 14 %, roofed 10 %, Gini of goods 8 % (tuning: the floors are judgement, as the redrawn tie-break stream is the only spread the gate sees). Widening one needs its own entry here.
- **Gate B's first evaluation (2026-10-07):** FAILED. Walking fell 12.0 % in the valley and 11.5 % on the coast, against a 9 % tolerance; every other aggregate was within its tolerance, and every run passed its checks. Run with one approximation at a time (`--without-leisure-blocks`, `--without-household-view`), the household view alone passed every aggregate (walking +0.0 % and +2.8 %), and the leisure blocks alone did not (−11.0 % and −9.1 %). In the twenty-year valley over 120 days, the blocks moved time from the hearth to rest at home: the hearth 4.20 to 3.86 h a person-day, rest 3.53 to 3.97, walking 0.524 to 0.479. Detailed mode decides again after each session, and its softmax choice may then go to the hearth. A block lasting until the next boundary, followed by a choice that may pick the same leisure again, lengthens every run of the leisure first chosen. The blocks were redrawn (ADR-0011 §4 amended): a block lasts the geometric number of sessions Detailed mode would live before choosing something else, drawn from the chance with which the leisure was chosen; one that ends so makes the next decision pass that leisure over; one cut at a boundary or at its longest is followed by a decision made afresh. Time use came within 4 %: the hearth 4.35 h, rest 3.45, walking 0.507. **Gate B then passed** (20 runs, 1.2 minutes on four threads, every run passing its checks). The largest differences were walking −4.4 % in the valley and the Gini of goods −5.0 % on the coast; people, sleep, food, harvest and the roofed share were within 0.5 %. No tolerance was widened. With the first blocks and the view together, one 120-day run showed trading 24 times as long as in Detailed mode (0.048 h a person-day against 0.002); with the redrawn blocks it was 0.001, and Gate B has no aggregate for trade alone.
- **The dashboard with Accelerated mode's approximations (2026-10-07, slice W, `cd6bfeb`):** the fifty-year dashboard lived its five worlds in 50.0 minutes on four cores, beside other work (75.8 minutes at `269edf3`), and **passed** for the first time: four rows green and one amber. Worlds 1, 2 and 3 took 24 to 25 minutes, world 5 16, and world 4, a village under village fields, 50. **Population green:** all five keep ten or more after fifty years (93 to 104 people in four, 10 in world 4), the most growth 2.7 times the founders (world 1, year 49). Food prices green (grain asked 3 to 8 log points more before the harvest than after; stocks rose after every harvest), firm sizes green (108 to 204 workshops a world), the Gini of goods amber (0.23 to 0.45; four of five below 0.3, as hoe farmers with land to spare may be). **Structural failures green:** 5 failures of lived-in buildings in 4,548 building-years, 1.10 per 1,000, against at most 2. All five were hut roofs whose rafters broke in storms, three of them poorly made, and none killed anyone; 6 empty buildings gave way besides. At `269edf3` the count was 11 in 4,635 building-years (2.37). Counts of 11 and 5 are within chance of one rate of about 1.7, and the slice's time-boxed tuning found nothing in the row to tune: it was spent reading the failures (the dashboard's JSON report now keeps their words). World 4 grew to 95 people and fell to 6 in year 43 (*NUDGE* below).
- **NUDGE: a village of 95 leaves together (2026-10-07, world 4 of the dashboard above):** under village fields, its harvests were 23.9 t in year 41 and 15.7 t in year 42, against about 22 t its 95 people eat in a year. On 14 May of year 43 food ran short with about two days' left, and within two weeks 17 households gave up and left; six people stayed, and ten lived there at year 50. The households-fields worlds of the same dashboard grew through such years. This is *villages of about 55 leave together* (§9, the M3b demo) at a larger size, and the regime may matter: whether the settlement's allocation by need keeps up with a village's growth has not been traced. Revisit with M4's councils, which give a village other answers to a short year than leaving. The chronicle now names the settlement a household leaves; before, it read "gave up and left ." in the observer and in `chronicle`.
- **The M3c demo (2026-10-07, plan §7):** river valley seed 2, the seed of the M3a demo, 768 cells, a band of 40, lived eight years from the command line (`civ-host run`) three times. The seed's weather is the same in every run, and `civ-host weather --seed 2` shows that it holds a dry year 4 (616 mm against 797 usual; 188 mm from April to July, the crop's season) and a wet year 5 (1,058 mm). The seed was the earlier demos', and was not picked for how its village fared. A year in the report runs from 1 March, so each holds its calendar year's harvest. **The dry year:** the crops had 57 to 58 % of the water they needed (a harvest 0.69 to 0.71 of an average year's). The village reaped 8.9 to 9.3 t of grain (502 kg/ha in the first run), against 16.5 to 18.7 t the year before. Its grain stores fell from 13.4–15.3 t to 10.8–12.7 t, and grain was asked at 1.36 to 1.42 hours of work a kilogram, against 1.14 to 1.20 the year before. Nobody went short of food and nobody left: about a year's grain in store carried them. **The wet year:** 1,146 mm in the report's year, the crops had 90 to 91 % of their water, and the village reaped 19.4 to 20.0 t (962 kg/ha in the first run). Stores rose to 17.8–19.7 t, and grain was asked at 1.14 to 1.16 again. The next years were wet too, and stores reached about 30 t by year 8. `web/e2e/m3c-demo.spec.ts` (with `TCE_DEMO=1`) makes the village to 1 November of year 5 and shows it in the observer: the weather panel's years against what they usually bring (year 4 "dry", 616 against 797 mm), the market with 11.8 t of grain offered by 11 households, and the chronicle's months that stood out. It then lives on at Max, the clock standing at a midnight each time it shows. Run once, it lived to year 41 at Max before the run stopped, with 71 people. Then fifty years in Accelerated mode passing the dashboard: the entry above.
- **The dashboard on M3c's last build (2026-10-07, `8093d51`, with nothing beside it):** passed in 31.7 minutes on four cores. Four rows green and the Gini of goods amber (0.26 to 0.33). All five worlds kept their bands, ending with 80 to 92 people (85 and 92 under village fields), the most growth 2.4 times the founders (world 4, year 48). Grain was asked 3 to 9 log points more before the harvest than after, and stocks rose after every harvest. **Structural failures:** 7 in 4,574 building-years, 1.53 per 1,000, every one a hut roof whose rafters broke in a storm, none killing anyone; no empty building gave way. No world fell as world 4 of the run before did. Worlds took 15 to 24 minutes each. Over the two runs of slice W's builds, 12 failures of lived-in buildings in 9,122 building-years, 1.32 per 1,000.
- **M4 in three parts (2026-10-07):** M4's contents need about five decisions expensive to reverse: the polity with its offices and laws, ties and standing, incidents and cases with their obligations, factions and episodes, and interventions that touch beliefs. That is more than one milestone's three ADRs (§1 rule 7). M4 is split into M4a (standing and the first council), M4b (crime and order) and M4c (factions and unrest), each with the usable bar and a demo of its own; M4's demo is M4c's. The council comes first because crime needs offices to answer it and unrest needs institutions to contest.
- **M4a design (2026-10-07, plan §7):** three research briefs (governance and law; crime and order; factions and unrest), with an observer summary, drawn from about thirty reports, their key citations checked against the reports. Three slices, X to Z (§7), and two ADRs: [ADR-0013](decisions/0013-polity-offices-laws.md) (the polity, its offices and its laws) and [ADR-0014](decisions/0014-ties-standing-notables.md) (ties, standing and notables). Choices that depart from the plan's first sketch:
  - **Scored moves, not HTN (amends §4.2).** Deliberation is one level deep: moves that a person's powers and the present issues allow, scored by their forecast effect on that person's goals. HTN planning waits for multi-step plans (M7). Issues only make moves available; no issue carries a weight toward a policy, as "a levy follows a famine" would be plot.
  - **One founding custom for every world.** A gathering of adults deciding by acclamation, where anyone may propose, and which may amend itself. Different regimes must come from history, not from a drawn starting form.
  - **Influence from ties held by others (amends §4.2).** §4.2 ranks notables by office, wealth, followers, founder status and reputation. Here standing is the esteem others' ties hold, by domain and audience; office and wealth count only through acts people saw.
  - **Notables are a compute tier.** They deliberate weekly and others when something reaches them. The flag grants nothing and is read only by the scheduler, and a gate checks it against every adult deliberating (ADR-0014 §4). §4.2's 1 % gets a floor, so a village of a hundred has several.
  - **Leaving becomes a scored choice.** Today a household leaves when food runs short. In slice Z it weighs staying, with what it expects from its fields, its stores, those it can ask and any relief, against going.
  - **Settled now for M4b and M4c:** no corporal punishment or execution in v0; the god tool's whispers are true claims only; an agitator is a newcomer sent with an ideology, not a person rewritten.
- **Scale at 1,000 people (2026-10-07, measured before slice X):** a band founded by `bench_village` on seed 2 (1,024 cells) at Max. 300 people lived a day in 111 ms; 1,000 lived one in 653 ms over their first ten days and 811 ms by days 21 to 30, about four minutes a simulated year, growing faster than the population. A profile (callgrind, 1,000 people, the second week) put 35 % of the time in route searches (about 720 a day at 1.7 million instructions each): the route cache holds 50,000 routes and is cleared whole when full, as well as monthly. 25 % was in scoring candidates, 11 % in trade's search for a deal, 9 % in `exp`, and 5 % in looking for deposits along each walk, which tests every exposed deposit against every segment of the route. Slice X works on these, exactly.
- **Slice X, scale (2026-10-07, M4a, `ce69c6f` and the commit after):** time-boxed exact speed work, each change proven by digests of every save section over 30 days at Max from a 1,000-person and a 2,000-person save (`bench_village --load … --max --digest`). **Changes:**
  - Route searches and travel fields keep Tobler's speed of each step in a per-thread table for the grid; the ground under a grid never changes, so a step is worked out once.
  - A search's scores sit side by side in one array.
  - The routing view is also kept a byte per cell, so a trail factor is one look-up.
  - Finding deposits along a walk first rules out bodies beyond the walk's box.
  - A deal's own costs are worked out only once an offer is worth pricing.
  - The route cache keeps two generations rather than emptying itself when full.

  **Measured over thirty spring days:** 1,000 people went from 1,674 to 1,354 ms a day, 2,000 from 5,256 to 4,278. **A whole year** of 1,000 people founded at once lived in 485 s at Max (1.33 s a day): 0.58 s in the first month, 1.5 s in spring, 2.2–2.6 s in the harvest weeks and 0.8–1.0 s in winter. The goal was two minutes, so it is **not met**. **Why:** on the slowest days route searches are 86 % of the time (callgrind). There are few of them (250–800 misses a day, half of them the way back of a cached route, with no route unreachable), but each grows costly once trails are worn: the search's lower bound assumes a trail's speed everywhere while most ground is walked off-trail at 0.6 of it, so a search expands 12,000–16,000 cells. A tighter bound (landmarks, or a bound by region) would cut that several-fold, but it can change which of two equally fast routes is walked, so it is not exact. It waits until a demo needs towns of thousands (M5, M6), with a one-time change of digests checked for equal route times. **Decisions** cost the rest of the time, spread thin: the deal search 12 %, patches 4 %, jobs 3 %, and `own_costs` 56,000 times a day at 2,000 people. Time grows faster than the population: 2,000 people cost 3.2 times 1,000. M4's own demos found villages of tens to hundreds, which this does not limit.
- **Ties, as built (2026-10-07, M4a slice Y's first step, ADR-0014 §1-2):** each person keeps up to 48 directed ties (content API 30, `[ties]`). A tie holds familiarity, warmth, good and bad evidence in four domains (provision, craft, word, counsel; counts that fade toward a prior of 1, research 04-06 §5.2), fear (0 until M4b), a balance of help in hours, and the act that last moved it, when and how many times running. Every quantity fades at its own half-life, worked out when the tie is next touched. Ties are written only at the acts the engine records:
  - a gift in answer to an ask: the asker writes provision and help received, in hours of the receivers' own work; the giver's elder writes the asker familiarity and help given;
  - wages paid: the worker writes word for the owner's elder, who writes craft for the worker;
  - rent: the tenant who threshed writes word for the holder's elder, and back;
  - a trade: buyer and seller's elder each write a little word;
  - learning beside someone: craft, by the hour;
  - company at the hearth: familiarity and warmth, by the hour.

  The household's elder stands for it: its eldest of an age to keep a household. At the hearth, each session touches three of those present from other households, a block of sessions three for each. 15 % of that company comes from people not yet known, the rest from those known, the better known the likelier. A first version weighted every face present, known ones up to 5 times, and at 1,000 people strangers filled every draw: 227,070 ties were let go in 30 days. With the share it was 16,888 (04-04 §1.5: people keep up the ties that matter). With no room, the least salient tie (familiarity plus warmth plus a tenth of its evidence) without more than 4 hours of help owed either way is let go. Ties to the dead and gone are let go on the first of each month. Nothing reads a tie yet, so **every other section of a save is byte for byte as before** (30 days of the 1,000-person save, `bench_village --digest`; only the content's fingerprint and the new section differ). Saves schema 28 (the `ties` section; an older save starts with none). **Measured:** a band of 40 after a year holds 32 ties a person, 36 of 42 people holding some, none let go; the reasons are the hearth 1,067 and trades 94. 1,000 people after 30 days hold 46.8 each, nearly all full, and let go about one tie in two a person a day. 30 days at 1,000 people take 1,385 ms a day, against 1,354 without ties.
- **Standing and notables, as built (2026-10-08, M4a slice Y's second and third steps, ADR-0014 §3-5):** on the first of each month, after ties to the gone are let go, each settlement's standing is summed from its adults' ties (the people profile's `[standing]`, content API 30). **Standing:** each adult's esteem by domain is the sum of the net good evidence the settlement's other adults hold for them. **Influence:** how many adults count them among the eight people they regard most, regard being esteem in every domain with warmth to tell apart those esteemed alike. ADR-0014 §3 had "among the few they esteem most in some domain"; one ranking over all domains with warmth was simpler and keeps company from counting as esteem. **Notables:** the most influential, 1 % of adults and at least three, ties broken by esteem and then id. A notable keeps the place while ranked within 1.5 times their number. The table is saved beside the ties (schema 28, an optional field; a save without it works it out afresh). Nothing reads the notable flag; deliberation, and the gate that checks the tier against every adult deliberating (ADR-0014 §4), come with slice Z. **The first choice that reads ties:** a household short of food asks, among those who could give it as much, the nearest once a walk is weighed against how well its members regard the one who stands for each household. They would walk `ask_known_min`, 30 minutes (a tuning value), further to ask someone they regard fully than a stranger. **The observer (wire 1.26):** the inspector lists someone's twelve strongest ties with the kernel's reasons ("kept them company at the hearth 35 times, last in April of year 1") and their standing; `GetStanding` and a Standing panel show each settlement's notables and those most counted, with the ties kept and let go across the world. **Measured:**
  - **Two bands of 40, three years at Max:** 31–34 ties a person, none let go. The reasons were hearth 877 and 1,042, trades 180 and 198, wages and work seen 7 and 26 each, rent and land lent 13 and 0, and gifts 3 and 0. Every adult was esteemed by someone, and the most influential was counted by 15 of 25 adults and 19 of 26.
  - **Ten-year smoke:** passed; 9 of 10 bands kept 43–63 people, and ria coast 5 died out in year 4, as before.
  - **Gate B:** passed with the same figures as before slice Y. Its fixture years see no shortage, so nobody asks for food. It does not yet compare ties.
  - **Tests:** the end-to-end suite passes (15 tests, the demos skipped), with a new standing test.
- **The polity and its first law, as built (2026-10-08, M4a slice Z's first step, ADR-0013 §1-5):** each settlement has a polity of its own id, founded at the first midnight under the custom every world shares: its adults decide by acclamation at the hearth, more for than against, a quarter of them needed, a tie failing (the people profile's `[polity]`, content API 31). The body is saved with the polity as values. **Issues:** food short, present when the settlement's food ran short in the last year or a household's food will not last until its next harvest. ADR-0013 §5 names "a food shortfall against the outlook"; a settlement-wide shortage alone never arose in five-year runs of three seeds. **Deliberation:** once a week, when nothing is before the gathering and an issue is present, the notables and the elders of the households whose food will not last each weigh proposing each level of each template that answers the issue (the new `policy` kind; the core pack's common store at a twentieth, a tenth or a fifth), against proposing nothing. A move is worth the person's forecast for their household, plus half the regard-weighted forecast for those who regard them, times the share of those they know who would back it, less a cost; a softmax picks. **The forecast:** the change in the expected log of a household's year of food above subsistence (half a year's food), over an ordinary year and a lean one at half the harvest. A lean year is believed one in four before any is seen; lean years seen are those the settlement's food ran short in. It pays the levy on its harvest and draws relief up to a year's need from a store holding the levy on an average harvest. A household near the edge gains and one with plenty loses, so a store splits a village by its fortunes (research 09-17 §1.2). **The gathering:** the next evening, for two hours from an hour before sunset. Attending is a new activity (`behavior = "attend"`, adults only), worth a point plus half of what the household and regard for the sponsor have at stake, beside the company. Every adult hears of it: awareness by word is left for M4c. At the next midnight each member who came takes a stance from their household's forecast and their regard for the sponsor, against a margin; the body decides; if it passed, those who came know the law. **Word of a law:** households tell their members at midnight, and companions at the hearth tell each other. **The levy:** at threshing, a thresher who knows the law pays its share of the grain their household keeps into the store, as their choice. They cannot when paying would leave the household below subsistence. Otherwise the chance of paying is logistic in the custom's pull (1.5 points), their stance (±1) and regard for the sponsor, less what paying costs their household; there is no sanction until M4b. **Relief:** the store, kept unroofed at the hearth, is one more place a household short of food may ask, by a law one of its members knows: at most five days of food an ask. The polity is a ledger holder (channels `levy` and `relief`), and every good stays accounted for. **History:** every law keeps its sponsor, issue and level, the stances of those present with what moved them, the decision and the counts, who knows it and since when, and what was paid, could not be, kept back or not known of, and given. Nothing is pruned (saves 29). The chronicle says each proposal and decision in words ("The gathering at Stonewick turned down a common store, taking a fifth of each harvest: 1 for, 18 against; 28 of 28 adults came."). **Measured:**
  - **Three seeds, four years:**
    - Seed 1: a twentieth passed in year 3, 20 for and 3 against, with 23 of 24 adults present.
    - Seed 2: a twentieth passed in year 1, 14–0.
    - Seed 3: a fifth was turned down 1–18, and a week later another sponsor's twentieth passed 27–0.
    - Afterwards: 300–1,100 kg levied a year, with 5–43 % of what was owed kept back or unpayable (the most in seed 2, whose poorer households could not pay). No relief was asked for, since no household ran short.
  - **Ten-year smoke:** all 10 worlds passed, and with them the new checks: one polity a settlement, and every law decided by its body's rule.
    - Every village ended with a common store at a twentieth, after 1–15 proposals (the smoke records how many, not at which levels). The levels were the same everywhere, but the timing, the number of failed proposals and the payments differed.
    - Each store took 1.8–6.8 t over the years, and 0.4–2.5 t was kept back or unpayable.
    - Four stores gave 143–286 kg in relief. The rest gave none, because nobody there ran short.
    - The unroofed stores lost a third to two-fifths of what came in to spoiling (32–40 %), and held 1.1–3.8 t at the end.
    - Ria coast 5, which died out in year 4 before this step, ended with 15 people, after its store gave 143 kg. The other nine kept 44–59 people.
    - **An observation, no nudge made:** every village converging on the smallest levy is a finding to watch, not a target. With one template, and a forecast that prices risk only through the lean-year belief, the twentieth is the level most households can back. Offices (Z4) and leaving as a choice (Z3) change what a store is for.
  - **Gate B:** passed (20 runs, 1.1 minutes, every run passing its checks). The largest differences were walking −4.9 % in the valley and work +6.3 % on the coast. The coast's Detailed spread of work grew to ±0.27 h a person-day from the ±0.15 its tolerances were calibrated at (its "calibrated" column reads 15.7 % for work against a 10 % tolerance); the cause was not traced. Gate B does not yet compare the polity.
- **The government panel, as built (2026-10-08, M4a slice Z's second step, ADR-0013 §7):** `GetGovernment` answers with each settlement's polity (wire 1.27): its custom in the kernel's words ("The adults who come to the hearth decide by acclamation: more for than against carries it, and a tie fails; a quarter of them must come."), its adults, what its store holds, the gathering called if any, and every law proposed there, newest first. Each law comes with its whole history: what it is, its sponsor and the issue it answered, the day of its gathering, the decision in words ("agreed: 14 for, 1 against; 25 of 25 adults came, 7 needed"), every stance of those who came with the points that moved it and why in words ("for: it makes little difference to their household, but they think well of the sponsor"; the sponsor's reads "they proposed it"), how many living people know it, and what its levy and the store's relief came to. The observer fetches it once a simulated day and opens a law to its history on a click. **Measured:** the end-to-end suite passes (16 tests, the demos skipped), with a new government test on river valley seed 2 lived 300 days. In its runs a fifth was turned down 1–21 and then a twentieth agreed 14–1, all 25 adults coming. In the one run whose stances were read in full (11 for, 1 against), most of those for it said their household gained little or lost, and backed it for their regard for the sponsor. **Found on the way:** a world's tie-breaks come from its identity, drawn afresh at each `civ-host new`, so two worlds of one seed differ in their details (the counts above varied between runs). This is by design (ADR-0011 §5's tie-break streams), not new; tests that need one world fix its identity.
- **Leaving weighed, as built (2026-10-08, M4a slice Z's third step):** before, a household out of food (less than a day's), worn down (its members had drawn on average `leave_at_depletion` of their reserve) and with no crop of its own ripening within 30 days or reaped and waiting, left with `leave_per_day`'s chance a day. Those conditions stand; the chance is now `leave_per_day` times the logistic of points for going less points for staying (the people profile's `[household]`, content API 32). **Going:** `leave_w_gap` (6) for the whole of the wait to its next harvest that staying would not cover. What covers it is its own food, its share of what the settlement's other households could spare (split among those out of food), and the common store's food if one of its members knows a common-store law in force, in days of its need against the days to its next harvest. **Staying:** `leave_w_stake` (3) for a whole year's food its fields should bring, which leaving gives up, and `leave_stay` (2) before either. With these tuning values a household with nothing to wait on leaves at 0.98 of the old rate, one that others or the store could carry to its harvest at 0.12 of it, and one with nothing in hand but a whole year's harvest coming at 0.73. The chance never exceeds the old rate. **Measured:** the ten-year smoke (on the build with the next step) passed all 10 worlds. Ria coast 5 was down to 7 people in year 3 and ended with 8 (15 in slice Z's first step, dying out in year 4 before it; worlds of one seed differ by run). Lived alone for four years, its third year's harvest was 0.67 of an average year's after 0.82 the year before; 22 of 42 people left in that year, though the store answered 167 asks with 312 kg. The store held 167–323 kg against what 42 people eat, and the levy could not be paid 264 and 393 times in years 2 and 3, so there was little to wait on: leaving ran near the old rate, as weighed. Whether a richer store keeps such a village is untested; the levy levels people chose are small (below).
- **The fifty-year dashboard on the build with leaving weighed (2026-10-08, before the next step, beside other work):** worlds took 790–1,974 s. Population green (all five keep ten or more; 67, 87, 91, 76 and 99 people at year 50; the most growth 2.5 times the founders), food prices green (grain asked 3–8 log points more before the harvest than after; stocks rose after 159 of 160 harvests), the Gini of goods amber (0.21–0.35), firm sizes green. **Structural failures red:** 9 failures of lived-in buildings in 4,470 building-years, 2.01 per 1,000 against at most 2, every one a hut roof whose rafters broke in a storm. Across the four recorded runs the counts were 11, 5, 7 and 9 (1.76 per 1,000 pooled), so the threshold sits inside the row's spread by chance; nothing was tuned. **Deaths:** 6 of the 9 killed someone (7 people), where both of slice W's runs killed nobody in 12. A time-boxed probe found no change: the collapse code is the same; at midnight, when buildings are checked, 99.9 % of people stood within reach of one of their household's buildings in both builds (3 seeds, 3 years each); and in slice W's saved run 3 of its 5 failures were huts their households had moved out of (20–57 m from home), while the 2 at home killed nobody, about even odds with four asleep at 0.15 each. The deaths are the designed risk for those under a roof that falls (ADR-0009 §5), not a regression.
- **Keeping the store, as built (2026-10-08, M4a slice Z's fourth step, ADR-0013 §2 in a first form):** a common store in force that holds food and has nobody keeping it is a new issue, `store_unkept`. It makes proposing a keeper a move under a new policy kind, `keep_store` (`core:policy/keep_store`, content API 33; `levy_shares` and `relief_days` now belong to `common_store` alone). **Nominating:** a deliberator's move names the adult of a sheltered household they regard most, themselves counted at full regard. Each household weighs its share of a year's spoilage saved under a roof (the store's food that would keep a year roofed but not in the open), as the change in the log of its year of food above subsistence; stances weigh regard for the one named. **The office:** the law names its holder. While they live in the settlement and their household is under a roof, the store spoils as a roofed store does and a household short of food asks for relief at the keeper's home. The goods stay the polity's on the ledger, apart from the keeper's household (ADR-0013 §4). **Succession:** when the holder dies or leaves, the law lapses (a new status, saved), the chronicle says so (in words like "Ada no longer keeps the common store at Stonewick: they died."), the store is unkept again, and a new proposal answers the issue. Nobody is heir to it (research 09-03 §3.1: early farming leadership can be task-specific; §5.1: an office has a holder and a vacancy state). **A deviation from ADR-0013 §2:** the ADR's Office primitive (a title, seats, eligibility, a selection procedure, removal, powers, pay and tenure as conditions) is not built. The first office is a law naming its holder, so it reuses the law pipeline's proposal, gathering, knowledge and history whole. Selection is the sponsor's nomination confirmed by the gathering, tenure lasts while the holder is resident, and there is no removal, pay or power over who gets relief. The general primitive waits until a second office, or M4b's sanctions, need seats, removal or pay. Saves keep the holder on the law (schema 30), and a later office section can be derived from such laws on load. **The observer (wire 1.28):** a law carries its holder, and a polity its offices in the kernel's words ("Storekeeper: Ada, since 3 May of year 2"; a lapsed one says how its holder went). The Government panel lists them, and the chronicle and panel name a keeper law "Ada as keeper of the common store". **Measured:** the ten-year smoke passed all 10 worlds. Every village named a keeper after its store, the store's food spoiled 11–18 % of what came in (32–40 % when nobody kept any, slice Z's first step), and stores ended holding 0.8–10.5 t. Eight villages kept a twentieth; two kept a tenth. Four stores gave 77–357 kg in relief. In ria coast 5 lived alone, Rilla was named keeper 25–0 in year 2. In year 3 that law lapsed, the store lay unkept, and Brisa proposed themselves and was named 13–0. Gate B passed (20 runs, 1.1 minutes, every run passing its checks); the largest differences were walking −4.9 % in the valley and work +6.5 % on the coast, whose Detailed spread of work stays at ±0.28 h a person-day, as after the first step. The end-to-end suite passes (16 tests, the demos skipped) with the panel's offices list.
- **Labels, as built (2026-10-08, M4a slice Z's fifth step, ADR-0013 §6):** `civ_sim::labels` works out what each polity would be called from its saved history alone, and nothing is saved. **The evidence** covers the last 24 months, or since the founding if the polity is younger (research 09-15 §4B: 24 months; test 12–36). De jure: the share of the governed adults its body admits. De facto: the matters it decided and how many failed for want of a quorum; the mean share of the body that came; the laws passed and who put them forward; of those who backed the leading sponsor's laws, how many would have gained too little for their households to back them, so that regard carried them; laws passed before the window that still bind; the levies paid and kept back under the levies in force (those unable, or who did not know, did not refuse: 09-01 §6.4); and the offices its laws created, with how many have held each. **The classifier** is a short decision tree over that evidence, with 09-15 §2's vocabulary. A body admitting at least 90 % of the adults is a council community (binding collective decisions, leadership answering to an assembly; 09-15 §4B's broad-franchise display threshold); one admitting half to 90 % is a council community qualified by its share; one admitting less than half is an oligarchy. One sponsor of at least two thirds of at least three laws passed sets the agenda (09-15 §4B's dominant-controller tag). If regard carried at least half of that sponsor's backers, the name adds big-man leadership (09-15 §2, after Sahlins); otherwise it adds "led by its proposer". **Modifiers:** its custom not yet exercised; few come (a mean turnout below half); each office, and "that outlived its holder" once a second holder was named (ADR-0013 §6's "whether authority outlived its holder"); and its levy mostly paid (80 % or more), often kept back (half or more) or mostly kept back, judged only from ten cases up (09-15 §6.5: a small denominator is unobserved, not compliance). **Confidence** is n/(n+2) for n decisions with a quorum in the window plus laws still binding from before, at least 0.2. Every threshold is a display convention or tuning value, uncalibrated. **Nothing reads it:** the people live in `civ-agents`, which cannot see `civ-sim`. A test lives a village five days twice from one save, working out every label and the government panel daily on one copy, and every save section ends byte for byte the same. **Not built:** 09-15 §4B's six-month label persistence, which would need saved state, and comparisons with published datasets, which do not apply to a hearth gathering. Membership other than all adults does not exist yet, so the oligarchy branch is reached only in tests. **The observer (wire 1.29):** a polity carries its label, modifiers, reasons and confidence. The Government panel shows "Would be called: Council community: a storekeeper's office; its levy mostly paid (confidence 0.67)", opening to the reasons in words. The run report adds a line per settlement, the smoke note the name and confidence. The dashboard's Regimes row lists each world's labels and stays ungraded: every world keeps its founding custom, which none can yet amend or seize (M4c), so labels that differ would show offices and evidence, not who rules. **Measured:** the end-to-end suite passes (16 tests, the demos skipped), its government test now also opening the label to its reasons. In its world (river valley seed 2, 300 days) the label read "Council community", with a storekeeper's office and a levy owed 9 times, too few to judge. **The ten-year smoke** passed all 10 worlds, and every village ended labelled a council community at confidence 0.50. None had decided anything in its last two years; its store and its keeper's law still bound. Each proposed 2–16 laws and passed 2–3. In three villages a keeper was gone and a successor named, which the classifier reads as an office that outlived its holder. A first run of this smoke, before laws still in force counted as evidence, labelled every village at 0.20 and would have called a custom exercised years before "not yet exercised"; that was fixed. **An observation, no nudge made:** with one founding custom and two policy templates, every village gets the same name. Labels that differ wait for histories that change who decides (M4c).
- **The notables' gate, as built (2026-10-08, M4a slice Z's sixth step, ADR-0014 §4):** the notables' tier is a run setting (`Sim::set_notable_tier`, never saved; a loaded world has it on). With the tier, the weekly review lets these weigh institutional moves: the notables, and those something reached since the last review (the elders of households whose food will not last, and, new in this step, whoever came to a gathering or learnt a law). Without it, every adult does. **The gate** (`civ-host notables`) founds Gate B's two fixture worlds with a fixed identity, lives their first month by the minute and saves them. From each save, five runs with the tier and five without live at Max to the end of year 3, each with its own tie-break stream and each checked at every year's end as every long run is. Four polity aggregates are compared: laws proposed, laws passed, offices filled, and the share of the food reaped that the polity levied or gave in relief. A difference of means is taken as a share of the larger of the mean with the tier and a floor (one law; one percent), since counts this small need one. **The first calibration and a failure:** with the tier as slice Z's first step built it (notables and households running short only), eight runs with the tier proposed nothing in river valley 1. Ria coast 2 proposed 3.6 ± 0.7 laws, passed exactly 2 and filled exactly 1 office in every run, its polity moving 0.4 % of the food reaped. Tolerances were fixed from that before any run without the tier: proposals 40 % and moved 65 % (three standard errors, rounded up); laws passed and offices filled, which did not vary, 40 % (two runs in five differing by one, a tuning value). The gate then FAILED: without the tier the coast filled 1.6 offices against 1.0, and passed 2.6 laws against 2.0. The runs' notes showed why. In three of five runs without the tier, Ulmer named themselves keeper on day 971, died, and Oren was named on day 1027. With the tier, others were named (Wren, Calla, Pell, Lomas) and still held the office. **The fix followed the ADR, not the tolerances:** ADR-0014 §4 lets everyone else deliberate "when something reaches them: a gathering they attend, a law they learn, or their household running short", and the first step had built only the last. With all three, a new calibration (eight runs with the tier) gave the coast 4.0 ± 1.1 proposals, 2.25 ± 0.46 laws passed, 1.25 ± 0.46 offices and 0.3 % moved; in two of the eight, Ulmer's path appeared with the tier too. The same rule (three standard errors, rounded up to the next 5 %) set the tolerances at 55 %, 40 %, 75 % and 25 %. **The gate passed** (20 runs in 1.6 minutes, every run passing its checks). On the coast: proposals 3.8 against 4.0 without the tier (+5 %), laws passed 2.4 against 2.6 (+8 %), offices 1.4 against 1.6 (+14 %), moved 0.3 % against 0.4 % (+8 %). River valley 1 proposed nothing either way, so it shows only that the tier invents nothing there. **What it does not show:** five runs of one coast world, whose runs share most of their history, are a thin sample; the gate guards against a tier that suppresses what every adult would do, not against every difference. At a village's size the tier saves nothing measurable: runs took 18.4 s (valley) and 18.8 s (coast) on average either way.
- **A sponsor remembers the vote (2026-10-08, M4a slice Z's sixth step, content API 34):** the first run of the M4a demo voted 27 times on a fifth or a tenth, each turned down (1–20 to 1–24 for a fifth), before a twentieth passed in its second year. Mira proposed 14 of them and Vane 7, and nothing a sponsor saw at a gathering entered what they expected the next. Now one who came to a gathering that decided the same proposal (the same policy at the same level, or naming the same holder) within `vote_memory_days` (a year, a tuning value) expects no more support for it than it got there (research 09-05 §1.2: a sponsor weighs expected success). A sponsor whose household stands to gain enough may still try again, since a proposal is worth its forecast times the support expected. It changed nothing in the notables' gate's fixtures (the same calibration, run for run).
- **The M4a demo (2026-10-08, plan §7):** river valley seed 2, the earlier demos' seed, which draws a dry year 4 (§9, the M3c demo), at 768 cells with a band of 40. It was lived five years from the command line twice (`civ-host run --seed 2 --size 768 --years 5`) and to 1 November of year 5 in the observer (`web/e2e/m4a-demo.spec.ts` with `TCE_DEMO=1`), whose screenshots show the Government panel with the label and a law opened to its history, the Standing panel, the chronicle and the village. Runs differ (each world draws its own identity). **Run 1:** in the lean weeks before the first harvest, on 30 August of year 1, Arrel proposed a common store at a twentieth "because food would not last until the harvest". The gathering agreed 9–1, 23 of 25 adults coming. The store lay unkept until year 2's harvest, when Fara proposed Mira as keeper (16–0, 17 of 23 adults). Over five years the levy was paid 1,715 times (2.4 t), kept back 180 times and could not be paid 331 times. In the dry year 4 the village reaped 9.2 t against 18.5 t the year before; nobody went short, the store gave no relief, and it held 1.4 t, then 2.2 t at the end. **Run 2:** Vane proposed a twentieth on 23 August of year 1 (21–1) and themselves as keeper three weeks later (24–0); year 4 brought 8.8 t against 18.4 t, again with no relief and 2.3 t in store at the end. **The observer's run** (the final build): a twentieth passed at its first gathering, 10–1 with 24 of 25 adults coming, and Arrel proposed Jask as keeper (18–0). Opened, the law shows its history: the sponsor; nine who backed it, eight of them saying their household stood to lose but they thought well of the sponsor; Rilla against, their household standing to lose; and thirteen who abstained. The village's notables were Imma, Hild and Loren, each counted by 15–16 adults among those they esteem most, mostly for their word. At 1 November of year 5 it would be called a council community, with a storekeeper's office and its levy mostly paid (91 % of those able and knowing of it paid), at confidence 0.50: it had decided nothing in its last two years, and its two laws still bound. An earlier observer run, before a sponsor remembered the vote, voted 27 times on a fifth or a tenth before a twentieth passed (the entry above). **What it shows:** institutions held by people, from a custom every world shares, whose history can be read. Here the store was kept and paid into but gave nothing in the dry year, because households' own stores carried them. That is what the village did, and it is what is shown.
- **The dashboard on M4a's last build (2026-10-08, `db4b45b`):** passed in 47 minutes on four cores, a test run beside it for part of the time. Four rows green and the Gini of goods amber (0.25–0.39). **Population:** all five worlds keep their bands, ending with 77 to 94 people, the most growth 2.4 times the founders (world 3, year 44). **Food prices:** grain asked 2 to 9 log points more before the harvest than after, and stocks rose after all 157 harvests. **Firm sizes:** 128 to 226 workshops a world. **Structural failures:** 2 in 4,630 building-years (0.43 per 1,000), both hut roofs in storms, neither killing anyone; no empty building gave way. Over the five recorded runs: 11, 5, 7, 9 and 2 failures. **Regimes (ungraded):** every world ended a council community whose storekeeper's office had outlived a holder, its levy mostly paid.
- **Opinion, as built (2026-10-08, M4c slice AG step one, ADR-0016 §4):** no new ADR. **A position per question:** a policy template may ask a question (`question`, content API 41: "whether to keep a common store"; four of the core templates do), and every adult holds a position on it, 0 against to 1 for, with a salience (1 while a law of it is in force or before the gathering, 0.3 otherwise). **The anchor** is what the household's own lot makes of the policy: on the first of each month the sponsor's forecast (the same one a sponsor weighs, refactored out of the polity review so both read it) is worked out for the law in force, or else the template's middle level, and passed through a logistic of one point; the position is then pulled toward it with a two-year half-life (research 06-04 §1.4, §3.2: Friedkin–Johnsen anchoring; without anchors opinions collapse to consensus, §2). **Talk at the hearth:** companions say where they stand, each question by a keyed chance at its salience, and a listener closes a share of the gap, η 0.03 times salience times their regard for the teller times a smooth disagreement gate (ε 0.25), doubled under 30 (06-04 §3.2's priors); no negative influence in v0. **At a gathering** the stance adds two points per unit that talk has moved someone from their household's lot, so a member's own gain still decides most stances, and the law's record keeps that part and says "talk at the hearth had drawn them toward it" (or turned them against it) when it mattered. **Measured, and changed:** the first build told 14 to 36 positions a person a week, against 06-04 §3.2's 0.5 (0.05–5); the chance a session was cut to 0.0015, and the smoke measured 0.46 to 1.85 told (0.33 to 1.62 taken in) a person a week, with talk moving positions 0.003 to 0.007 from their anchors on average: talk shades opinion, it does not make it. **A latent flaw of slice AF surfaced:** with stances a little more spread, overruled members began to amend the custom, and two worlds cycled, 51 amendments (50 passed) and 8 (8 passed) in ten years. An amendment's forecast had re-decided every decision a household saw, amendments included and those made under earlier customs, so each change gave the other side cause to change it back. It now counts only the laws and cases decided under the present custom, never an amendment. **The ten-year smoke then passed all 10 worlds:** one village amended its custom once (coast 2, to its households' elders, a quarter of whom must come; labelled an oligarchy, 0.75) and none cycled; about 50 people hold 104 to 136 positions. The smoke's own check of each law's body now reads the custom in force on the day it was decided, and counts a superseded amendment as having passed. **Boundary:** saves schema 37 (36 still loads: positions are taken on the next first of the month), wire 1.36 (each person's positions in the inspector, the talk part of a recorded stance), content API 41. **Gates:** Gate B and the notables' gate passed. **Not built:** values, ideologies and norms (the rest of slice AG); confidence and redundancy in taking talk in (06-04 §1.4's b·z term is the monthly pull); positions of children; and positions that weigh in anything but a gathering's stance.
- **Norms, as built (2026-10-08, M4c slice AG step two, ADR-0016 §4):** no new ADR. **A norm is content** (`kind = "norm"`, content API 42): what it says, what the kernel does with it (`does`; only `abide_by_laws` is built) and the priors its states are drawn from; the core pack's `core:norm/gathering_binds` says that what the gathering decides binds everyone. **Each person's state** (research 06-05 §1.1, §5.1–§5.2): an endorsement, drawn by a key on the norm's content id and pulled toward the mean of their parents' as the objection to taking is (log-odds mean 0.4, spread 1, heritability 0.5); a belief of the share of households that abide, which founders start at 0.85 (the custom they brought) and someone new takes from their household; and a threshold drawn evenly from 0.2 to 0.9 (06-05 §2.2), with a smooth step a tenth wide. Each takes theirs on the first of the month, in id order so parents draw first, or when first asked to pay a levy. **What it does:** paying a levy or keeping a curfew the gathering passed weighs one point for a full endorsement and one for a belief well above one's threshold (06-05 §5.2: μV + γA), and the people profile's flat `comply_base` falls from 1.5 to 0: the founders' village starts near the old weight (about 0.58 + 0.93), and from then each person's own state carries it. In a test village, a store's levy was paid 0.91 of the times it was decided where everyone held the norm firmly and believed every household paid, and 0.59 where nobody did. **What moves belief:** only what companions at the hearth say of their own household's last levy (the share of the season's threshings it paid; a household that could not pay is not counted, as the custom does not ask what a household has not got), each by a keyed chance of 0.001 a session, closing a tenth of the gap (06-05 §2.2: 0.02–0.30); never the settlement's true rate. **The ten-year smoke passed all 10 worlds:** endorsement 0.52 to 0.60 on average; people believed 0.90 to 0.95 of households abide where 0.86 to 0.95 had paid at their last levy; 0.82 to 0.96 were held to it by others' doing it; 0.08 to 0.29 accounts a person a week (06-05 §2.2's learning window is 8 to 32 informative encounters). The norm is stable here because levies are mostly paid: it amplifies a lean year's keeping back only weakly (with these priors a change in belief moves paying about a fifth as much, worked out from the logistic and the spread of thresholds rather than measured, so no spiral), recorded and not tuned. Three villages amended their custom once each, none cycled. **Also in this build:** grievances and hearing records of the dead and departed are now let go each midnight (slice AE kept them: a coast world of 10 people reported grievances held by 28; it now reports 6 held by 2). **Boundary:** saves schema 38 (37 still loads: everyone takes a state at the next first of the month), wire 1.37 (each person's norms in the inspector), content API 42. **Gates:** Gate B and the notables' gate passed. **Not built:** the normative expectation (what others think one ought to do; nothing yet says what anyone approves of), sanctions for breaking a norm, people hiding what their household kept back, and gossip of other households' acts; norms of other kinds, among them sharing in need, which waits for asks a household can refuse at will; and norms that supply a grievance's expectation (ADR-0016 §2: grievances still arise only against a law's terms).
- **Values, as built (2026-10-08, M4c slice AG step three, ADR-0016 §4):** no new ADR. **A value is content** (`kind = "value"`, content API 43); the core pack names three after research 06-04 §1.1's list (security, autonomy, reciprocity): safety from want and harm, a household's say over what is its own, and giving back what was given or taken. **Each person's** is drawn between -1 (less than most) and 1 (more than most) as `tanh(mean + sd z)`, by a key on the value's content id, a child taking half its parents' mean (06-04 §1.7: transmission with variation), and kept for life (§3.2's twenty-year drift toward experience is not built). The core priors are a mean of 0 (the core pack tilts nobody one way), a spread of 0.8 and a weight of one point: design priors. **A policy template says how it bears** (`[bears]`, -1 to 1, an authoring judgement): a common store for safety (1) and giving back (0.5) and against a household's say (-0.5); a law against taking for giving back (1) and safety (0.5); a watch for safety (1) and against a household's say (-0.5); a curfew for safety (0.5) and against a household's say (-1). **What a law does to what someone holds dear** (their values times its bearing, at each value's weight) adds to their anchor on its question beside their household's lot, to their stance at its gathering (the record keeps that part and says "what they hold dear drew them toward it" or "turned them against it"), and to how a sponsor weighs proposing it.
